Two-sided cartesian fibrations of synthetic ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -categories
Jonathan Weinberger

Abstract
. Within the framework of Riehl–Shulman’s synthetic ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -category theory, we present a theory of two-sided cartesian fibrations. Central results are several characterizations of the two-sidedness condition à la Chevalley, Gray, Street, and Riehl–Verity, a two-sided Yoneda Lemma, as well as the proof of several closure properties. Along the way, we also define and investigate a notion of fibered or sliced fibration which is used later to develop the two-sided case in a modular fashion. We also briefly discuss discrete two-sided cartesian fibrations in this setting, corresponding to ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -distributors. The systematics of our definitions and results closely follows Riehl–Verity’s ∞ \infty -cosmos theory, but formulated internally to Riehl–Shulman’s simplicial extension of homotopy type theory. All the constructions and proofs in this framework are by design invariant under homotopy equivalence. Semantically, the synthetic ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -categories correspond to internal ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -categories implemented as Rezk objects in an arbitrary given ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -topos. MSC2020 : 18N60, 03B38, 18D30, 18N45, 55U35, 18N50 Keywords: ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -categories, two-sided cartesian fibrations, Segal spaces, Rezk spaces, fibered Yoneda Lemma, homotopy type theory, simplicial type theory



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Synthetic higher category theory

1.1.1.
\infty
-Cosmos theory

In recent years, there have been ongoing investigations into higher category theory using
synthetic
approaches where the basic objects already have intrinsic homotopical meaning. The program of Riehl–Verity
[
68
]
develops
(\infty,n)
-category theory, for
0\leq n\leq\infty
, using the notion of
\infty
-cosmos
1
1

1

The terminology has been suggested by Peter May
[
68
, Acknowledgments]
, after
[
85
]
.
which provides a semi-strict way to present
(\infty,2)
-categorical universes of a wide range of notions of higher categories (e.g. quasi-categories, Rezk spaces/objects,
n
-complicial sets,
\Theta_{n}
-spaces, …)—as well as derived notions thereof such as slices, discrete objects, fibrations,
etc
. This has sparked a very powerful program to establish
\infty
-category theory in model-independent way. Intriguingly, they demonstrate how parts of the theory can even be reduced essentially to (strict)
2
-categorical arguments
[
73
,
71
]
which makes precise a range of very helpful intuitions that previously had lacked justification at this level of conceptionality, rigor, and comprehensiveness. Indeed,
\infty
-cosmos theory systematically employs techniques from
Australian
or
formal category theory
to work internal to sufficiently complete
(\infty,2)
-categories that are thought of as universes for different notions of higher categories.

1.1.2.
Simplicial type theory

While
\infty
-cosmos theory is based on traditional set theory, there also have been approaches to higher category theory within various
type theories
. A type theory is a formal system that allows for doing mathematics in a more
structuralist
way, as opposed to the
materialist
nature of set theory. The advent of homotopy type theory and univalent foundations (HoTT/UF) due to Voevodsky
[
93
]
, Awodey–Warren
[
6
]
,
et al.
has led to a new area of research yielding synthetic accounts to homotopy theory. This provides a significant generalization of the pioneering earlier work by Hofmann–Streicher
[
37
,
38
]
on the (
1
-)groupoid interpretation of Martin–Löf type theory. Roughly, the basic objects in HoTT are homotopy types,
i.e.
spaces up to homotopy
aka
weak
\infty
-groupoids, cf.
[
41
,
88
]
. In fact, after various works towards extending the class of models such as
[
78
,
79
,
80
,
24
]
Shulman was able in 2019 to prove Awodey’s conjecture that any
(\infty,1)
-topos (in the sense of Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie
[
64
,
49
]
) admits a model-categorical presentation that supports a full model of HoTT.

This does not yield an account to synthetic higher categories right away. Indeed, defining a notion of
(\infty,1)
-category in plain HoTT has been a long standing open problem.

Thus, variations of HoTT have been proposed that indeed make it possible to reason about higher categories using (complete) Segal-type formalisms such as
two-level type theory

[
19
,
4
]
based on Voevodsky’s
homotopy type system (HTS)

[
92
]
. Another such approach is
simplicial (homotopy) type theory
, introduced in
[
66
]
.
2
2

2

Cf.
[
17
]
for a high-level overview.
There, one adds another layer of strict simplicial shapes, containing e.g. the directed cubes
\mathbbn{2}^{n}
, standard
n
-simplices
\Delta^{n}
, the
(n,k)
-horns
\Lambda_{k}^{n}
,
etc
. Type families can then also depend on shapes. Another feature is the notion of
extension type
: given a shape inclusion (“cofibration”)
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
, a type family
A:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
, and a partial section
a:\prod_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}A
, there exists the type

\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\Phi}}{\textstyle\prod_{\Phi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\Phi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\Phi}}A\middle|^{\Psi}_{a}\right\rangle

of sections
b:\prod_{\Psi}A
satisfying
a(t)\equiv b(t)
for all
t:\Phi
.

Together with the given shapes, this induces a sensible notion of (dependent)
hom-type

\hom_{A}:A\to A\to\mathcal{U}
, as well as synthetic versions of the Segal and Rezk conditions. Riehl–Shulman’s work on discrete fibrations in this setting was later generalized to the case of co-/cartesian fibrations
[
18
]
and lextensive
(bi-)fibrations

[
97
, Chapter 4]
. The text at hand presents a further generalization of the one-sided (co-/)cartesian case to the
two-sided
cartesian case.

1.2.
Co-/cartesian families

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a universe type, cf.
[
91
, Section 1.3]
. A
cocartesian family

P:B\to\mathcal{U}
over a
Rezk type

B
(
aka
a synthetic
(\infty,1)
-category) precisely captures the idea of a
functorial
type family valued in (
\mathcal{U}
-small) synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories: an arrow
u:a\to_{B}b
in the type
B
induces covariantly a functor
u_{!}^{P}\equiv:u_{!}:P\,a\to P\,b
between the fibers:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leadsto}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{!}}

Dually, a
cartesian family

P:B\to\mathcal{U}
transforms
contravariantly functorially
,
i.e.
an arrow
u:b\to_{B}a
induces a functor
u_{P}^{*}\equiv:u^{*}:P\,a\to P\,b
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}

These notions generalize the familiar
1
-categorical concepts of a Grothendieck op-/fibration
[
89
]
to the synthetic higher setting. Fibrations of
(\infty,1)
-categories have initally been studied by Joyal
[
39
]
and Lurie
[
49
]
for quasi-categories, and consecutively very notably by Ayala–Francis
[
7
]
, Barwick–Shah
[
11
]
, Barwick–Dotto–Glasman–Nardin–Shah
[
10
]
, Rezk
[
63
]
, Cisinski
[
23
]
, Nguyen
[
56
]
, Cisinski–Nguyen
[
25
]
, as well as other authors. Particularly crucial for our approach are the treatments by Rasekh
[
61
,
62
]
for Rezk spaces, and Riehl–Verity’s
\infty
-cosmos theory
[
68
,
69
]
which generalizes concepts and results due to Street
[
86
,
87
,
84
]
and Gray
[
32
]
to a rigorous and powerful framework of model-independent higher category theory.

In the context of simplicial type theory, Riehl–Shulman have considered
covariant families

[
66
, Section 8]
, functioning as a synthetic variant of (discrete) left fibrations
[
39
]
.

1.3.
Two-sided cartesian families

Two-sided cartesian families are type families
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
which fibrationally are presented by
spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\xi
is cocartesian,
\pi
is cartesian, and some compatibility conditions between the two respective liftings are satisfied. An instructive example is given by the “hom span”
\partial_{1}:A\leftarrow A^{\Delta^{1}}\to A:\partial_{0}
of a Rezk type
A
, and from ensuing properties one also obtains comma spans
\partial_{1}:C\leftarrow f\mathbin{\downarrow}g\rightarrow B:\partial_{0}
induced by a span
g:C\leftarrow A\to B:f
of functors.
3
3

3

In fact, these are even
discrete
two-sided fibrations, cf.
[
66
, Section 8.6]
,
[
68
, Section 7.2]
,
[
47
, Theorem 2.3.3]
.
Originally, this notion goes back to Street
[
86
,
87
,
84
,
47
]
, and it has been generalized and thoroughly investigated for
\infty
-cosmoses
[
68
, Chapter 7]
. Semantically, two-sided families correspond to
categorical
(\infty,1)
-distributors
,
i.e.
bifunctors
A^{\Op}\times B\to{\mathrm{Cat}}
into the
(\infty,1)
-category of small
(\infty,1)
-categories.
4
4

4

Even though this cannot be expressed in our theory yet, cf.
[
97
, Chapter 7]
.
The significance for
\infty
-cosmos theory is that the discrete variant,
i.e.
the
(\infty,1)
-distributors or
modules
, form a
virtual equipment

[
28
]
, a rich double-categorical structure that presents the formal
\infty
-category theory of an
\infty
-cosmos. The Model Independence Theorem states that a biequivalence between
\infty
-cosmoses lifts to a biequivalence of the associated virtual equipments.
5
5

5

Note the parallel to axiomatic homotopy theory where a Quillen equivalence between “homotopy theories” presented through model categories lifts to an equivalence of their associated homotopy categories.
Here, however, we will deal with the categorical two-sided case. Namely, we will provide a structured analysis, leading up to characterizations and closure properties generalizing the one-sided case. This follows the thread of of
[
68
, Section 7.1]
, but with a more explicit account of various (auxiliary) notions of fibered (or
sliced
) fibrations, owed to the lack of categorical universes in the present theory. Our treatise nevertheless often times make use of techniques from “formal” category theory, by reasoning about the various conditions in terms of statements about (fibered) adjunctions, and their closure properties. We view this as a fruitful pratical effect of the
\infty
-cosmological philosophy on the synthetic theory formulated in simplicial type theory.

Our treatise ends with a two-sided Yoneda Lemma, and a (very brief) note on discrete two-sided families.

1.4.
Structure of the paper

A two-sided cartesian family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
corresponds to a span
\xi:A\leftarrow E\rightarrow B:\pi
with
\xi
a cocartesian and
\pi
a cartesian fibration such that the lifts are compatbile in a certain sense. But such a family or span, resp., can also be understood as a
cartesian
functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

between cartesian fibrations that in addition is a
fibered
or
sliced cocartesian fibration
over
B
. By duality and the Chevalley criteria for (ordinary) co-/cartesian fibrations and functors, resp., there are several equivalent way to express this fact, cf.
Theorem

4.8
, after
[
68
, Theorem 7.1.4]
.

Semantically, the condition is that
\varphi:\pi\to_{B}q
is an object of the
\infty
-cosmos
\mathrm{co}{\mathcal{C}}\mathrm{art}(\mathcal{C}\mathrm{art}(\mathcal{K}))
for the ambient
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{K}
(which is of the form
\mathcal{K}=\mathscr{R}ezk_{\mathscr{E}}
, cf.
[
68
, Proposition E.3.7]
for an
\infty
-cosmos
\mathscr{E}
presenting an
(\infty,1)
-topos.).

Section

2
is a technical introduction and a recap of the basic fibrational theory set up in
[
18
]
. We then work towards two-sided cartesian families by first discussing sliced cocartesian families in
Section

3
, in particular their characterizations à la Chevalley and their closure properties, cf.
Section

3.2
. Next, we specialize to the case of cocartesian families in cartesian families, cf.
Section

3.3
.

Section

4
then introduces and the notion and establishes basic properties of two-sided cartesian families and fibrations, resp. In
Section

4.1
we provide the basic formalism of two-variable families and bifibers. Finally, in
Section

4.3
we define two-sided cartesian families and prove a number of Chevalley-like characterization criteria.

In
Section

5
we introduce two-sided cartesian functors and prove several closure properties (a subset of which corresponds to the
\infty
-cosmological closure properties, cf.
[
68
, Section 7.2]
).

We proceed by proving a two-sided cartesian Yoneda Lemma in
Section

6
and conclude by briefly discussing discrete two-sided cartesian families in
Section

7
, namely how they can be understood as discrete objects in the
\infty
-cosmos of two-sided cartesian fibrations.

Helpful technical results about several fibered constructions and notions are provided in the Appendix. We treat fibered equivalences in
Appendix

A
and fibered (LARI) adjunctions in
Appendix

B
. In
Appendix

C
we conclude with preservation properties for sliced commas and products.

1.5.
Contribution

We develop a theory of two-sided cartesian fibrations
[
86
,
87
,
84
,
69
,
68
]
of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-category theory in simplicial homotopy type theory
[
66
]
. The central results are a characterization theorem (
Theorem

4.8
), a Yoneda Lemma (
Theorems

6.5
and
6.6
), and several closure properties (
Theorem

5.18
). Along the way, we also investigate other notions like sliced co-/cartesian families or cocartesian-on-the-left families.

In doing so we work completely internally in simplicial HoTT, so all the results externalize to theorems about internal
(\infty,1)
-categories in an arbitrary given Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie
(\infty,1)
-topos.

Specifically, our work in this type-theoretic setting generalizes previous developments from
[
66
]
and
[
18
]
following ideas, concepts, and techniques from
[
69
,
68
]
and translating into the type theory at hand.

The work presented here appears as
[
97
, Chapter 5]
. This PhD thesis was written at TU Darmstadt under the supervision of Thomas Streicher.

1.6.
Related work

Two-sided cartesian fibrations internal to
2
- and bicategories have been introduced by Street
[
86
,
87
,
84
]
, cf. also the review in
[
47
]
. They have been later generalized to the
\infty
-cosmological setting by Riehl–Verity
[
69
,
68
]
. Our work is an adaption of the latter, in particular
[
68
, Chapter 7]
, to the setting of simplicial homotopy type theory
[
66
]
. This generalizes previous work on (one-sided) co-/cartesian fibrations
[
18
]
in this setting. Moreover, there exist treatments of lextensive
[
98
]
and exponentiable
aka
Conduché
aka
flat fibrations
[
9
]
in this setting.

Recently, large parts of Riehl–Shulman’s initial paper
[
66
]
, including the
\infty
-categorical Yoneda Lemma
[
45
]
have been formalized
[
45
]
in Kudasov’s new proof assistant
Rzk

[
44
]
. There have been various follow-up formalizations in
Rzk

[
76
]
. These currently do not include the work at hand about two-sided cartesian (non-discrete) families but we this development to be possible depending some more groundwork formalizations.

By the semantics of simplicial HoTT
[
66
,
77
,
99
]
in
(\infty,1)
-toposes of the form
\mathscr{E}^{{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op}}
for an
(\infty,1)
-topos
\mathscr{E}
, the synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories in our theory are interpreted as internal
(\infty,1)
-categories in
\mathscr{E}
,
i.e.
Rezk objects. Those have been investigated in various works and semantic settings, notably
[
69
,
68
,
62
,
15
,
82
,
51
,
53
,
50
,
52
]
.

Directed univalence for a universe of synthetic left fibrations has been shown by
[
21
]
. In a (bi-)cubical analogue to sHoTT, there is work by Weaver–Licata
[
96
]
on directed univalence for covariant discrete fibrations. There is also recent work on co-/limits
[
9
]
for (complete) Segal types in simplicial HoTT by Bardomiano Martínez.

In HoTT, univalent categories and the Rezk completion have been considered in
[
1
,
2
]
and
[
91
, Section 9.9]
. In two-level type theory (complete) (semi-)Segal types have been treated in
[
20
,
19
,
4
]
. Directed homotopy type theory with semantics in e.g. categories has been discussed by North
[
57
]
, and
bicategorical
directed type theory has been introduced by Ahrens–North–van der Weide
[
3
]
. Earlier, directed variants of type theories have been considered by Warren
[
95
]
and Licata–Harper
[
46
]
. Synthetic notions of variance has been studied by Nuyts
[
58
]
.

Perspectives on directed type theories in connections with synthetic higher categories and/or fibrations are given in
[
17
,
42
]
.

2.
Synthetic fibered
(\infty,1)
-category theory: A quick starting guide

2.1.
Homotopy type theory

The basic objects in dependent type theory are
families

A
of
types
, depending on
contexts

\Gamma
, indicated by the judgment
\Gamma\vdash A
. A context
\Gamma
is a finite list of variable declarations. So if
\Gamma\equiv[x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}]
, then
\Gamma\vdash A
means that
A\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is a family of types depending on the free variables
x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}
.

If
\Gamma\equiv[\,\,\,]
is the
empty
context then a dependent type
\Gamma\vdash A
is just a single, constant type
A
. A type
A
is inhabited by
terms
or
elements

a:A
.

If
\Gamma\vdash A
, we can consider the
extended context

\Gamma.A\equiv[x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n},y:A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})].

Given families
\Gamma\vdash A
and
\Gamma.A\vdash B
, there are two important new constructions. We can form
\Sigma
-types
or
dependent sum types

\Gamma\vdash\sum_{a:A}B(a)
whose elements are pairs
\langle a,b\rangle
with
a:A
and
b:B(a)
. If
A
is a constant dependent type, then
\sum_{a:A}B(a)
coincides with the cartesian product type
A\times B
.

Again, given dependent types
\Gamma\vdash A
and
\Gamma.A\vdash B
, we can form the
\Pi
-type
or
dependent function type

\Gamma\vdash\prod_{a:A}B(a)
. The elements are given by dependent functions, or
sections
,
x:\Gamma.A\vdash f(x):B(x)
, which can be thought of functions with “varying codomain.” In case
\Gamma
is the empty context, the
\Pi
-type
\prod_{a:A}B(a)
coincides with the ordinary function type
A\to B
.

Given a dependent term
x:\Gamma.A\vdash f(x):B(x)
we denote the corresponding dependent function via the
\lambda
-constructor

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.f(x):\prod_{a:A}B(a)
.

A crucial ingredient for homotopy type theory are Martin-Löf’s identity types: given a type
A
and terms
x,y:A
one can consider the
identity type
or
path type

(x=_{A}y)
(often just abbreviated as
(x=y)
) whose terms are witnesses or paths identifying the points
x
and
y
. There always exists the canonical reflexivity path
{\mathrm{refl}}_{x}:(x=_{A}x)
for
x:A
, and the identity types satisfy an induction principle (akin to the Yoneda Lemma) stating that the reflexivity terms inductively generate the family
x:A,y:A\vdash(x=_{A}y)
.

Two terms
x,y:A
being
judgmentally equal
,
x\equiv y
, entails that there exists an identification
p:(x=_{A}y)
, but in an
intensional
type theory (such as HoTT) the converse is not true.

We want to assume a (hierarchy of) universe(s)
\mathcal{U}
of all small types, implementing the idea of Grothendieck universes, well-known from category theory inside set theory. Though not material for most of our work, it is convenient to assume Voevodsky’s
univalence axiom
stating that in a canonical way the paths of the universes, as captured by its identity type, coincide with the weak equivalences,
i.e.
, bi-invertible functions between types.

Univalence yields a straightening-unstraightening construction on the level of types, see
Theorem

2.1
. Namely, we can understand a dependent type
\Gamma\vdash A
either as family
A:\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}
, where
\Gamma\equiv A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n}
or as a “fibration”
\sum_{a:A}B(a)\to A
.

Indeed, by path induction, one can show that any type family
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
behaves like a fibration in the topological sense in that it supports a
path transport
operation
(-)_{!}^{P}:\prod_{a,b:A}\prod_{u:(a=_{A}b)}P(a)\to P(b)
. For a path
u:(a=_{A}b)
and points
x:P(a)
,
y:P(b)
, the path type
(u_{!}^{P}(x)=_{P(b)}y)
gets identified with the type
(x=^{P}_{u}y)
(short:
(x=_{u}y)
) of
paths over
u
,
i.e.
, paths
\varphi:(\langle a,x\rangle=_{\sum_{x:A}B(x)}\langle b,y\rangle)
such that
\mathrm{pr}_{1}(\varphi)=u
. In this way, we can think of type families also supporting
path lifting
, in line with the topological intution.

In this sense the family
x,y:A\vdash(x=_{A}y)
can be identified with a
path space fibration

\mathrm{Path}(A)\to A\times A
in a model category
[
6
]
.

More background on homotopy type theory can be found in e.g.
[
91
,
74
,
65
,
5
,
33
]
.

2.2.
Simplicial homotopy type theory

Simplicial (homotopy) type theory (s(Ho)TT) has been introduced by Riehl–Shulman
[
66
]
as an extension of Martin–Löf type theory (MLTT) or homotopy type theory (HoTT), resp., to reason synthetically about
(\infty,1)
-categories. Building on this, there has been further work on directed univalence
[
21
,
96
]
, fibered
(\infty,1)
-category theory
[
18
,
97
]
, and co-/limits
[
9
]
.

We give a short overview on how sHoTT can be presented, and on the basic concepts we will need in this theory. For a longer exposition of the basics of this theory we refer to the original paper
[
66
]
, and for a detailed discussion of the fibrational prerequisites we refer to
[
18
]
.

2.2.1.
Strict shapes

In usual dependent type theories, a type family
A
depends on a
context

\Gamma\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}[x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}]\vdash A.

This gets extended in simplicial type theory
[
66
]
where now types can depend on
shapes
that are defined as sub-polytopes of finite-dimensional cubes. One writes

\Xi\;|\;\Phi\;| \;\Gamma\vdash A

where
\Xi
denotes a
cube
, and
\Phi
a (sub-)
shape
of
\Xi
.

A general cube is of the form
\mathbbn{2}^{n}
, where
\mathbbn{2}^{1}\equiv\mathbbn{2}
denotes the directed bipointed linear order

(0\leq 1).

From this, one can define common shapes in the expected way, such as e.g.:

\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{t:\mathbbn{2}\;|\;t\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\},\quad\Lambda_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\},

\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\equiv t\lor s\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\}.

Strictness
of the cube and shape layer refers to the fact emethat equality of shape terms
\Phi\vdash s\equiv t
is reflected as definitional equality in the type level.
m
As explained in
[
18
, Section 2.4]
we also coerce the shapes to be types.

2.2.2.
Extension types

Let be a shape inclusion
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
(in some common cube context
\Xi
). We consider a family
P:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
and a
partial
section
a:\prod_{t:\Phi}P(t)
. Then we can form the
extension type

\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\Psi}}P\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle

consisting of all
total
sections
b:\prod_{t:\Psi}P(t)
such that
t:\Phi\vdash a(t)\equiv b(t)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

The rules for the extension type are similar to that of the usual
\Pi
-types but invoking the strict extension property, cf.
[
66
, Subsection 2.2]
. The intuition is that
\Phi\hookrightarrow\Psi
corresponds to a kind of cofibration along which we ask for extensions of a given section of the fibration
\Phi^{*}P\twoheadrightarrow\Phi
. This idea originally goes back to unpublished work by Lumsdaine and Shulman, and it has also been used e.g. in cubical type theory
[
27
,
59
]
to define path types
[
90
]
.

The extension types serve to define a notion of absolute and dependent hom-types, and analogous generalizations thereof to different shapes and higher cells. In particular, this gives rise to synthetic notions of
\infty
-groupoids
aka

discrete types
, pre-
(\infty,1)
-categories
aka

Segal types
, and
(\infty,1)
-categories
aka

Rezk
or
complete Segal type
. We briefly introduce and explain these notions in
Section

2.3
. For a comprehensive treatment we refer to
[
66
, Sections 5, 6, and 10]
and
[
18
, Section 2]
.

In general, we will freely switch back and forth between type families and fibrations, in particular when defining notions of these. E.g., defining the notion of a cocartesian
family
simultaneously introduces a notion of cocartesian
fibration
, and
vice versa
. For a formal justification, cf.
[
18
, Section 2.5]
. Specifically, for any family we have the following type-theoretic version of the Grothendieck construction
aka
straightening/unstraightening:

Theorem 2.1

(Typal Grothendieck constr., cf.
[
91
, Theorem 4.8.3]
)
.

For the types

\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{A,B:\mathcal{U}}A\to B,\qquad\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{B:\mathcal{U}}(B\to\mathcal{U})

and maps

\displaystyle\partial_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda A,B,\pi:A\to B.B:\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})\to\mathcal{U},

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda B,P:B\to\mathcal{U}.B:\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})\to\mathcal{U},

there is a fiberwise quasi-equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\St}

at stage
B:\mathcal{U}
given by a pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B\to\mathcal{U})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\St_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{B}}

with
straightening

\St_{B}(\pi)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\mathrm{fib}_{b}(\pi)

and
unstraightening

\Un_{B}(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\widetilde{P},\pi_{P}\rangle

(
\pi_{P}:\widetilde{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b:B}P\,b\to B
the total space projection).

In particular, this yields a weak equivlence of maps
\Un_{B}\circ\St_{B}
and
\id_{\mathcal{U}/B}
, so for any map
E\to B
we obtain a fibered equivalence, which we call the
projection equivalence
of
\pi
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{b:B}b^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}

2.3.
Synthetic
\infty
-categories

Recall the notion of extension type from
Section

2.2.2
. Importantly, this will serve to define a tractable notion of synthetic
\infty
-category.

Definition 2.2

(
\hom
-types,
[
66
, Definition 5.1]
)
.

Let
A
be a type with terms
x,y:A
. Then the extension type

\hom_{A}(x,y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\Delta^{1}\to A\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{1}}_{[x,y]}\right\rangle

is called the
hom-type
or
type of directed arrows
from
x
to
y
.
We also write

(x\to_{A}y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hom_{A}(x,y),

or simply
(x\to y)
if the underlying type is understood.

By the rules of extension types, a term
f:\hom_{A}(x,y)
is exactly a function
f:\Delta^{1}\to A
such that
f\,0\equiv x
and
f\,1\equiv y
.

To treat composites we have to consider diagrams of shape
\Delta^{2}
.

Definition 2.3

(
\hom^{2}
-types,
[
66
, Definition 5.2]
)
.

Let
A
be a type with terms
x,y,z:A
. Then, assuming we are given
f:(x\to_{A}y)
,
g:(y\to_{A}z)
, and
h:(x\to_{A}z)
(and leaving the endpoints implicit in the notation), we can consider the type

\hom_{A}^{2}(f,g;h)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to A\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{2}}_{[f,g,h]}\right\rangle.

By
Definition

2.3
, a Segal type
A
admits composition of arrows, uniquely up to homotopy in the following sense: given terms
x,y,z:A
, arrows
f:(x\to_{A}y)
and
g:(y\to_{A}z)
there is some composite arrow
h:(x\to_{A}z)
together with a
2
-simplex
\sigma_{f,g,h}:\hom_{A}^{2}(f,g;h)
witnessing that
h
is, in fact, a composite of
f
and
g
; and more so, the type of this data is contractible.

We are now interested in singling out those simplicial types which always admit composition of (composable) arrows, uniquely up to homotopy. These will be our synthetic pre-
(\infty,1)
-categories.

Definition 2.4

(Segal types,
[
66
, Definition 5.3]
)
.

A
Segal type
is a type
A
such that

\isSegal(A)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{x,y,z:A}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:(x\to_{A}y)\\
g:(y\to_{A}z)\end{subarray}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{h:\hom_{A}(x,z)}\hom_{A}^{2}(f,g,h)\Big).

One can show
[
66
, Theorem 5.5]
that this is equivalent to being homotopically right orthogonal to the horn inclusion
\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
, see also
Definitions

2.6
and
2.7
.

Based on the
\hom
-types we can define a notion of
isomorphism
.

Definition 2.5

(Isomorphisms)
.

Let
A
be a type. Let
x,y:A
and
f:\hom_{A}(x,y)
be an arrow. We say
f
is an
isomorphism
if and only if the proposition

\isIso(f)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Big(\sum_{g:hom_{A}(y,x)}g\circ f=\id_{x}\Big)\times\Big(\sum_{h:hom_{A}(y,x)}f\circ h=\id_{y}\Big)

is satisfied, where
\id_{x}\equiv\lambda t.x:\hom_{A}(x,x)
is the
identity
morphism of
x
.

The
type of isomorphisms
from
x
to
y
is then defined as

{\mathrm{iso}}_{A}(x,y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(x\cong_{A}y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{f:\hom_{A}(x,y)}\isIso(f).

In particular, the identity maps can be shown to be isomorphisms. By path induction, we then obtain a comparison map

{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{A}:\prod_{x,y:A}(x=_{A}y)\to(x\cong_{A}y),\quad{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{A,x,x}({\mathrm{refl}}_{x})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\id_{x}.

This connects the new notion of isomorphism with the natively given notion of equivalence through paths.

2.4.
Right orthogonal and LARI families

We will be concerned in our treatment with specific notions of fibrations, based on synthetic analogues of co-/cartesian fibrations, and occasionally also (discrete) left/right fibrations. However, for both conceptual and technical purposes it will be instructive to introduce the general patterns behind those definitions: right orthogonality and LARI/RARI conditions.

We remark that the notion of iso-/inner family is not so much of conceptual interest, but rather of technical convenience, cf. also the discussion at the beginning of
[
18
, Section 4]
.

2.4.1.
Directed arrows in type families

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a type family with
a,b:B
and
u:(a\to_{B}b)
. After
[
66
]
, we can then consider the type
dependent homomorphisms
or
dependent arrows
. For
x:P\,a
and
y:P\,b
, the type of (dependent) arrows in
P
over
u
is given by

\mathrm{dhom}_{P,a,b,u}(x,y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(x\to_{u}^{P}y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(x\to_{u}y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}P(t)\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{1}}_{[x,y]}\right\rangle.

Analogously, we can define dependent versions of
\hom^{2}
and any other possible shape inclusion. This naturally leads to relative versions of the Segal and Rezk conditions, which we will take as the basis of all of our following theory: we will consider isoinner fibrations/families over a Rezk base type. This means, the base type behaves like a category, and so will the total type as well as all the fibers, compatible with the canonical projection from the total type to the base. But these families need not satisfy lifting properties w.r.t directed arrows (though they do w.r.t. the ordinary path types from standard homotopy type theory, like any type family). The precise setting for (iso)inner families is given in
[
18
, Section 4]
, and we recall it briefly in the following.

2.4.2.
Fibrations and fibered maps

We are introducing the notation
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
for map between if we want to view it as a fibration of some kind. In writing so, we are somewhat imprecise in that it could mean different things: sometimes it just describes a map
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
between types, but to be viewed as a fibration in a slice category over
B
, in the model. This is emphasize its role somewhat akin to to that of an (axiomatic)
isofibration
in the sense of
\infty
-cosmos theory
[
68
]
. E.g., when considering a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

of maps between types, really all maps occuring could be written as
\twoheadrightarrow
(by projection equivalence). But the intuition is that a square like this encodes a fibered map
\langle k:B\to A,\varphi:(b:B)\to P(b)\to Q(k(b))\rangle
(where
P
and
Q
, resp., denote the straightening of
E
and
F
, resp.), so we do not regard
k
and
\varphi
as fibrations (but rather, as a fibered functor between fibered categories.
6
6

6

Though we will soon also consider certain fibered functors (between fibrations placed in the vertical direction) that can be regarded as fibrations themselves (placed in the horizontal direction).

Sometimes
\twoheadrightarrow
might specifically mean that we are considering a (co)cartesian fibration or some related notion, which will be made explicit by the context.

2.4.3.
Right orthogonal families

Definition 2.6

(Right orthogonal family,
[
18
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a family. Given a type map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
, we say that
P
is
right orthogonal to
j
or a
(right)
j
-orthogonal family
, written
j\bot P
, if and only if the proposition

\prod_{v:X\to B}\prod_{f:(t:Y)\to P(v\,t)}\isContr\Big(\sum_{g:(t:X)\to P(v\,t)}j^{*}(g)=^{P}_{v}f\Big)

is satisfied.

In case
B\simeq\mathbf{1}
, the family
P
can be identified with a single type
A
. In this case we say that
A
is
right orthogonal to
j
and write
j\bot A
.

Let
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
denote the projection associated to a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
. Then
P
is
j
-orthogonal if and only if the type of fillers
g
in any diagram as follows is contractible:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

In case of a shape inclusion
j:\Phi\hookrightarrow\Psi
a family
P
is right orthogonal to
j
if and only if

\prod_{v:\Phi\to B}\prod_{f:(t:Y)\to P(v\,t)}\isContr\Big(\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\Psi}}P\middle|^{\Phi}_{f}\right\rangle\Big),

cf.
[
18
, Section 2.4]
.

Definition 2.7

(Iso-/inner family,
[
18
, Definitions 4.1.1, 4.2.3]
)
.

(1)

A type family is
inner
if it is right orthogonal to the horn inclusion
\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
. A
Segal type
is a type that is inner when seen as a family over the
\mathbf{1}
.

(2)

An inner family is
isoinner
if it is right orthogonal to the terminal projection
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbf{1}
, where
\mathbb{E}
denotes the
free bi-invertible arrow
, cf.
[
18
, Subsection 4.2.1]
. A
Rezk type
or
complete Segal type
is a type that is isoinner (again, when seen as a family over
\mathbf{1}
).

The Segal types are exactly the synthetic pre-
(\infty,1)
-categories
7
7

7

Cf.
[
1
]
and
[
91
, Section 9.1]
for a general discussion.
in our setting while the Rezk types are precisely the synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories.

The condition of being a co- or contravariant
discrete
family (meaning
\infty
-groupoid valued) can be naturally expressed in terms of a right orthogonality condition, as originally worked out in
[
66
, Section 8]
. Over a point this of course specializes to the type being a synthetic
\infty
-groupoid.

Definition 2.8

(Co- and contravariant families,
[
66
, Definition 8.2]
)
.

A family is
covariant
if it is right orthogonal to the shape inclusion
0:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
. Dually, it is
contravariant
if it is right orthogonal to the shape inclusion
1:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
.

Over a point, in either of these cases we call the ensuing type
discrete
.

2.4.4.
LARI families

Co-/cartesian families can be characterized in terms of certain adjoint existence criteria. Namely, a map
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
is a synthetic cocartesian fibration iff the Leibniz cotensor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

has a
left adjoint right inverse (LARI)
, cf.
[
66
, Section 11]
and
[
18
, Appendix B.1]
. If the latter map exists, it exactly functions as the
lifting map
, mapping a pair
\langle u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime},e:P\,b\rangle
to its cocartesian lift
P_{!}(u,e):e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}
{}{}{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

.

Formally generalizing this condition to any shape inclusion leads to the notion of
LARI family
or
LARI fibration
,
i.e.
, maps
\pi:E\to B
such that for a certain shape inclusion or type map
j:Y\to X
the following induced map has a LARI:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

LARI adjunctions in simplicial HoTT have been studied in
[
18
, Appendix B]
, extending the work on adjunctions in simplicial HoTT by Riehl–Shulman
[
66
, Section 11]
. This in turn extends the study of adjunctions in plain HoTT
[
91
, Chapter 4]
. In the semantic picture, Riehl–Shulman’s theory of adjunction can be understood to internalize parts of of Riehl–Verity’s theory of homotopy coherent adjunctions
[
70
]
. In particular, they show in
op. cit.
that a
2
-categorical adjunction extends to a fully homotopy coherent one. In
Appendix

B
we extend the setup of
[
18
, Appendix B]
by a fibered version of LARI adjunction.

Definition 2.9

(
j
-LARI family, cf.
[
68
, Cor. 6.3.8]
,
[
18
, Def. 3.2.2]
)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a shape inclusion or type map. A family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a
j
-LARI family
if for its unstraightening
\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\pi_{P}:E\to B
the following Leibniz cotensor map has a LARI:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}B^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

2.5.
Co-/cartesian families

2.5.1.
Cocartesian families

We only treat the covariant case. The contravariant case replaces the initial vertex
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
with the terminal one
i_{1}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
and left adjoint inverses (LARIs) with right adjoint inverses (RARIs).

Definition 2.10

(Cocartesian arrow,
[
18
, Def. 5.1.1]
, cf.
[
68
, Def. 5.4.1]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family over a Rezk type
B
. An arrow
f:e\to_{u}^{P}e^{\prime}
over an arrow
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}
is
P
-cocartesian
if and only if

{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{u}^{P}(f)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{b^{\prime\prime}:B}\prod_{v:b^{\prime}\to b^{\prime\prime}}\prod_{e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}}\prod_{h:e\to^{P}_{vu}e^{\prime\prime}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{g:e^{\prime}\to^{P}_{v}e^{\prime\prime}}h=_{vu}^{P}gf\Big).

Definition 2.11

(Cocartesian family,
[
18
, Def. 5.2.2]
, cf.
[
68
, Def. 5.4.2]
)
.

An isoinner family over a Rezk type
B
is
cocartesian
if and only if for
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}
and
e:P\,b
the type

\sum_{e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}}\sum_{f:e\to^{P}_{u}e^{\prime}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{u}^{P}(f)

is inhabited.

Given
P,u,e,e^{\prime}
as above, we will denote the subtype of cocartesian arrows as
(e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{P}_{u}e^{\prime})\hookrightarrow(e\to^{P}_{u}e^{\prime})
, and likewise for the actual arrows in the illustrating figures.

One can show that the inhabitedness condition from the above definition implies contractibility. In other words, cocartesian lifts of a given arrow in the base are determined uniquely up to homotopy by their source vertex.

Connecting back with
Definition

2.9
we can express being a cocartesian family as a LARI condition.

Theorem 2.12

(Chevalley criterion: Cocartesian families via lifting,
[
18
, Def. 5.2.6]
cf.
[
68
, Prop. 5.2.8(ii)]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family, and denote by
\pi:E\to B
the associated projection map. The family
P
is cocartesian if and only if the Leibniz cotensor map
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi:E^{\Delta^{1}}\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B
has a left adjoint right inverse:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

In
Theorem

2.12
the stipulated LARI acts as the
lifting map

\chi:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E^{\Delta^{1}}
with
\chi(u,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P_{!}(u,e):e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{P}_{u}u_{!}\,e
. It is also possible to state cocartesianness in terms of a
transport map

\tau:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to_{B}E
,
\tau(u,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}u_{!}\,e:P(\partial_{1}\,u)
. This is the content of the next theorem.

Theorem 2.13

(Cocart. families via transport,
[
18
, Thm. 5.2.7]
, cf.
[
68
, Pr 5.2.8(ii)]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
an isoinner family with associated total type projection
\pi:E\to B
.

Then,
P
is cocartesian if and only if the map

\iota\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota_{P}:E\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B,\quad\iota\,\langle b,e\rangle:\equiv\langle\id_{b},e\rangle

has a fibered left adjoint
\tau\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\tau_{P}:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E
as indicated in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The adjoint criteria from
Theorems

2.12
and
2.13
are of central importance for a variety of reasons. First, they imply a lot of closure properties, cf.
[
68
, Corollary 6.3.8, Proposition 6.3.14]
and
[
18
, Section 3, Proposition 5.3.17]
. Next, they are generalized in our present study to fibered and two-sided situations. Finally, from a broader perspective in model-independent higher category theory the adjoint criteria à la Chevalley, Gray, Street, are taken by Riehl–Verity as “reference definitions” for co-/cartesian fibrations internal to an arbitrary
\infty
-cosmos. It turns out that there is also an equivalent definition
[
68
, Definition 5.2.1]
directly on
2
-cells but the adjoint criteria are more fit to readily yield an internal definition.

In fact, recently Riehl–Verity showed that co-/cartesian arrows in the
\infty
-cosmological sense can also be characterized by Chevalley-type criteria, cf.
[
68
, Theorem 5.17]
. This does not play a role in the present text but has been discussed in
[
97
, Appendinx A.2]
within simplicial HoTT.
8
8

8

Generalizing to arbitrary shape inclusions or type maps again this gives rise to the notion of
j
-LARI cell
.

2.5.2.
Cartesian families

Dually to the cocartesian case and the left adjoint (right inverse) characterizations, we can consider cartesian fibrations and right adjoint (right inverse) characterizations. Similarly, this can be expressed as a (contravariant) lifting condition w.r.t.
cartesian arrows
.

Given a cartesian family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
over
u:b\to_{B}a
, for
e:P\,b
and
d:P\,a
, we denote by
(d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

the subtype of cartesian arrows. The cartesian lift w.r.t.

, where

is the cartesian transport of

by

.

2.5.3.
Cocartesian functors

We conclude our overview with a brief consideration of cocartesian functors in this setting. Those also turn out to be characterizable in terms of Chevalley criteria.
9
9

9

We obtain a formally analogue characterization via Chevalley criteria in the context of
j
-LARI cells and
j
-LARI-cell-preserving functors, cf.
[
97
, Theorem A.2.6]
.

Definition 2.14

(Cocart. functors,
[
18
, Def. 5.3.2]
, cf.
[
68
, Def. 5.3.2]
)
.

If
P
and
Q
are cocartesian families, and furthermore the map

\Phi:\widetilde{P}\to\widetilde{Q},\quad\Phi\,b\,e:\equiv\langle j(b),\varphi_{j}(e)\rangle

preserves cocartesian arrows, then we call
\Phi
a
cocartesian map
:
10
10

10

This is a proposition because being a cocartesian arrow is a proposition.

{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\Phi):\equiv\prod_{f:\Delta^{1}\to\widetilde{P}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{P}(f)\to{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{Q}(\varphi f).

In particular, if
B
and
C
in addition are Segal (or Rezk) types we speak of a
cocartesian functor
.

We define

{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B,C}(P,Q):\equiv\sum_{F:{\mathrm{Fun}}_{B,C}(P,Q)}{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(F).

Given families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
to
Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
, a
fibered functor
from
P\to Q
is a section
\varphi:\prod_{x:B}Px\to Qx
. It is cocartesian if

{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\langle\id_{B},\varphi\rangle)\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:\Delta^{1}\to B\\
f:\Delta^{1}\to u^{*}P\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{P}(f)\to{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{Q}(\varphi f).

We define

{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(P,Q):\equiv\sum_{\varphi:\prod_{B}P\to Q}{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\langle\id_{B},\varphi\rangle).

Theorem 2.15

(Characterizations of cocart. functors,
[
18
, Thm. 5.3.19]
, cf.
[
68
, Thm. 5.3.4]
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be Rezk types, and consider cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:A\to\mathcal{U}
with total types
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
and
F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{F}
, resp.

For a fibered functor
\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle j,\varphi\rangle
giving rise to a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

the following are equivalent:

(1)

The fiberwise map
\Phi
is a cocartesian functor.

(2)

The mate of the induced canonical fibered natural isomorphism is invertible, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}

(3)

The mate of the induced canonical natural isomorphism is invertible, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

2.5.4.
Closure properties of co-/cartesian families and functors

Proposition 2.16

(Cosmological closure properties of cocartesian families,
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.17]
)
.

Over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Cocartesian families are closed under composition, dependent products, pullback along arbitrary maps, and cotensoring with maps/shape inclusions. Families corresponding to equivalences or terminal projections are always cocartesian.

Between cocartesian families over Rezk bases, it holds that:
Cocartesian functors are closed under (both horizontal and vertical) composition, dependent products, pullback, sequential limits,
11
11

11

all three objectwise limit notions satisfying the expected universal properties w.r.t. to cocartesian functors
and Leibniz cotensors.

Fibered equivalences and fibered functors into the identity of
\mathbf{1}
are always cocartesian.

3.
Sliced cocartesian families

The main object of study of this paper are two-sided cartesian fibrations
E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
. It turns out to be instructive to analyze this notion by means of
fibered
or
sliced fibrations
,
i.e.
, fibered functors
\varphi
between fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\varphi
is regarded itself as a fibration of some kind, over the base
\pi
(itself a fibration). This is the point of view developed by Riehl–Verity in
[
68
, Chapter 7]
, and we adapt it here. For this purpose, we develop in this section the theory of fibrations fibered or sliced over a fibration. While the development undoubtedly presents itself as technical, it will prove instrumental in our main theorems about the characterization of two-sided cartesian families and their closure properties, see
Theorems

4.8
and
5.18
.

3.1.
Sliced constructions

We introduce here type-theoretic versions of a few sliced constructions, cf.
[
68
, Proposition 1.2.22]
.

Definition 3.1

(Sliced cotensor,
[
68
, Proposition 1.2.22(vi)]
)
.

Let
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
be a map, and
X
be a type or shape. The
sliced exponential (over
B
) of
\pi
by
X
is given by the map
(X{\multimap}E)\to B
defined as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X{\multimap}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This means
X{\multimap}E\simeq\sum_{b:B}X\to P\,b
.
In particular, for
X\equiv\Delta^{1}
, we obtain the
vertical arrow
type
{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)\to B
. We often identify vertical arrows
f:e\to^{P}_{\id_{b}}e^{\prime}
with
f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime}
.

Definition 3.2

(Sliced product,
[
68
, Proposition 1.2.22(vi)]
)
.

Let
I
and
B
be types, and consider maps
\pi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B
for
i:I
. The
sliced product
over the
\pi_{i}
is defined by pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{B}\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Definition 3.3

(Sliced comma,
[
68
, Proposition 1.2.22(vi)]
)
.

Consider a cospan
\psi:F\to_{B}G\leftarrow_{B}E:\varphi
of fibered functors, giving rise to the sliced comma type
\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\psi
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\times E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\times\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

3.2.
Sliced cocartesian families

Definition 3.4

(Sliced cocartesian families)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type. A
sliced cocartesian family over
B
is given by the following data:

•

an isoinner family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,

•

an isoinner family
K:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
,

•

and, writing
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Sigma_{\widetilde{P}}K
, a witness for the proposition
12
12

12

recall the uniqueness of cocartesian lifts, cf.
[
18
, Proposition 5.1.3]

\prod_{b:B}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:\Delta^{1}\to P\,b\\
x:Q(b,f0)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{\prime}:Q(b,f1)\\
k:x\to^{Q}_{b,f}x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{Q}_{f}(k).

We call
K
a
cocartesian family sliced over
B
with base
P
, and denote the ensuing cocartesian lifts as

K_{!}^{b}(f,x)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}K_{!}(b,f,x):x\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{K}_{\langle b,f\rangle}f_{!}^{K,b}\,x.

Perhaps more familiarly, in fibrational terms, a
cocartesian fibration sliced over
B
with base
\pi
is given by a fibered functor
\varphi:\xi\to_{B}\pi
, where
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow B
and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
are isoinner fibrations over
B
, visualized through

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

moreover satisfying the analogous lifting property: any
\pi
-vertical arrow has a
\varphi
-cocartesian lift.

As previously with ordinary cocartesian families, we will often bring in the fibrational viewpoint and reason diagrammatically.

This is a type-theoretic formulation of what, more generally in
\infty
-cosmos theory, defines for any
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{K}
and an object
B\in\mathcal{K}
a cocartesian family in the slice-
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{K}/B
. This is captured internally by the following theorem, which shows that the above condition precisely amounts to the sliced version of the familiar LARI condition for cocartesian families.

Theorem 3.5

(Characterization of sliced cocartesian families)
.

Given a Rezk type
B
, let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
K:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
be isoinner families. We write
Q\equiv\Sigma_{\widetilde{P}}K:B\to\mathcal{U}
, and denote

\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(P):E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}\twoheadrightarrow B,\quad\xi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(Q):F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{Q}\twoheadrightarrow B,

\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{E}(K):F\to E:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{Q}\simeq\widetilde{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Q}\equiv\pi_{\Sigma_{\widetilde{P}}\,K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{P}}

Then the following are equivalent propositions:

(1)

The family
K:E\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family sliced over
B
.

(2)

The sliced Leibniz cotensor
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}_{B}\varphi:{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi}(F)\to_{B}\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E
has a fibered LARI:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}_{B}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(3)

The fibered inclusion map
\iota_{\varphi}:F\to_{E}\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E
has a fibered left adjoint:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-ii}\implies\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-i}
:

We abbreviate
r\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}_{B}\varphi
. After the usual projection equivalence, we can identify it as the fiberwise map with components

r_{b}\big(f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime},k:x\to^{Q}_{f}x^{\prime}\big)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle e,f,x\rangle\equiv\langle\partial_{0}\,f,f,\partial_{0}k\rangle

for
b:B
.

Assume, the stated fibered LARI condition is satisfied.
First we note that the invertible unit, for every
b:B
, exhibits
\chi_{B,b}
as a (strict) section of
r_{b}
, given
e:P\,b
,
f:e\mathbin{\downarrow}P\,b
and
x:Q(b,e)
, we can assume
\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x):x\to^{Q}_{f}x^{\prime}
for some
x^{\prime}:Q(b,\partial_{1}\,f)
.
We have a fibered equivalence

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,b^{\prime}:B\\
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e,e^{\prime}:P\,b\\
f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime}\\
x:Q(b,e)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,d^{\prime}:P(b^{\prime})\\
g:d\to_{P\,b^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y:Q(b^{\prime},d),y^{\prime}:Q(b^{\prime},d^{\prime})\\
m:y\to^{Q}_{g}y^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots(\chi_{B}(e,f,x)\to_{u}\langle g,m\rangle)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\langle e,f,x\rangle\to_{u}\langle d,g,y\rangle).

Just as in the second part of the proof of
Theorem

2.12
, by specializing to the case that
m=\id_{x^{\prime\prime}}
and
g=\id_{e^{\prime\prime}}
for some
e^{\prime\prime}:P(b^{\prime})
and
x^{\prime\prime}:Q(b^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
, we find that the lift
\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)
is a
K
-cocartesian lift of the
P
-vertical
arrow
f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime}
.

\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-i}\implies\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-ii}
:

On the other hand, suppose that
K
-cocartesian lifts of all
P
-cocartesian maps exist, w.r.t. to a given initial vertex. Accordingly, we define
\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}K^{b}_{!}(f,x):x\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{f}^{K}x^{\prime}
. Again, analogously to the first part of the proof, we define a pair of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)\to_{u}\langle g,m\rangle\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\langle e,f,x\rangle\to_{u}\langle d,g,y\rangle\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

by

\displaystyle\Phi(h:e\to_{u}^{P}d,h^{\prime}:e^{\prime}\to^{P}_{u}d^{\prime},k:x\to^{K}_{h}y,k^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\to^{K}_{h^{\prime}}y^{\prime})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle h,h^{\prime},k\rangle

\displaystyle\Psi(h:e\to_{u}^{P}d,h^{\prime}:e^{\prime}\to^{P}_{u}d^{\prime},k:x\to_{h}^{K}y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle h,h^{\prime},k,\mathrm{fill}^{K}_{\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)}(m\circ k)\rangle.

Due to cocartesianness of
\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)
these are quasi-inverse to each other. In particular, the components of
\Phi
are defined by applying the right adjoint
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}_{B}\varphi
. For the unit of the adjunction, we take reflexivity, and taken together this defines a fibered LARI adjunction.

\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-i}\implies\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-iii}
:

The fiberwise map
\iota_{\varphi}:F\to_{E}\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E
is given by

\iota_{\varphi}(b,e,x)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle b,e,e,\id_{e},x\rangle.

Because of the preconditions we can define the candidate fibered left adjoint
\tau_{\varphi}:\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E\to_{E}F
by

\tau_{\varphi}(b,e^{\prime},e,f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime},x:Q(b,e))\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle b,e^{\prime},f^{Q}_{!}(x):Q(b,e^{\prime})\rangle,

as we would expect analogously to
Theorem

2.13
.
To obtain a fibered adjunction as desired, recalling
Theorem

B.1
,
Item

3
, we want to define a family of equivalences

\Phi:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
e^{\prime}:P\,b\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e:P\,b,x:K\,b\,e\\
f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{x^{\prime}:K\,b\,e^{\prime}}\Big(\underbrace{\tau_{\varphi}(e,f,x)}_{\equiv f_{!}x}\longrightarrow_{K(b,e^{\prime})}x^{\prime}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\Big(x\longrightarrow^{Q}_{b,f}x^{\prime}\Big):\Psi,

generalizing
Theorem

2.13
, by
13
13

13

Note that for the codomain of the equivalence we have identified the type of morphisms
\Big(\langle e,f,x\rangle\longrightarrow\langle e^{\prime},\id_{e^{\prime}},x^{\prime}\rangle\Big)
(in the fiber
\sum_{g:P\,b\mathbin{\downarrow}e^{\prime}}Q(b,\partial_{0}\,g)
) with simply
(x\to^{b^{*}Q}_{f}x^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle \Phi_{b,e^{\prime}}\big(m:f_{!}\,x\to_{Q\,b\,e^{\prime}}x^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}m\circ Q^{b}_{!}(f,e),

\displaystyle\Psi_{b,e^{\prime}}\big(k:x\to_{f}^{b^{*}Q}x\big)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{Q^{b}_{!}(f,x)}^{\varphi}(k),

see
Figure

1
for an illustration. By
Q
-cocartesianness
of the lifts of
P
-vertical arrows in
K
one can show—analogously to the proof of
Theorem

2.13
—that the maps are quasi-inverse to one another.

\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-iii}\implies\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-i}
:

By assumption, there exists a fibered functor
\tau_{\varphi}:\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E\to_{E}F
and a fibered natural transformation

\eta:\big(\id_{\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E}\Rightarrow^{K}_{E}\iota_{\varphi}\circ\tau_{\varphi}\big)\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
e:P\,b\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}d:P\,b\\
f:d\to_{P\,b}e\\
x:Q(b,d)\end{subarray}}\langle d,f,x\rangle\longrightarrow^{K\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E}_{\langle b,e\rangle}\langle e,\id_{e},f_{!}x\rangle,

where we write
f_{!}x
for the respective component, for the sake of foreshadowing.
Here,

K\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b,e.\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d:P\,b\\
f:d\to_{P\,b}e\end{subarray}}Q(b,d):E\to\mathcal{U}

is the straightening of
\partial_{1}:\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E\twoheadrightarrow E
. Note that there is an equivalence

\Big(\langle d,f,x\rangle\to_{(K\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E)(b,e)}\langle e,\id_{e},f_{!}\,x\rangle\Big)\simeq\Big(x\to^{b^{*}Q}_{\langle b,f\rangle}f_{!}\,x\Big),

as illustrated by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{!}\,x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x}}

Furthermore, by the assumption, the induced transposing map
14
14

14

again, identifying
\langle d,f,x\rangle\longrightarrow_{\langle v,g\rangle}\langle e^{\prime},\id_{e^{\prime}},x^{\prime}\rangle
with
x\to^{K}_{\langle v,gf\rangle}x^{\prime}
is a family of equivalences:

\displaystyle\Phi:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,b^{\prime}:B\\
e:P\,b\\
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}\\
g:e\to^{P}_{v}e^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}d:P\,b\\
f:d\to_{P\,b}e\\
x:Q(b,d)\end{subarray}}\prod_{x^{\prime}:Q(b^{\prime},e^{\prime})}\Big(f_{!}\,x\longrightarrow_{\langle v,g\rangle}^{K}x^{\prime}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\Big(x\longrightarrow^{K}_{\langle v,gf\rangle}x^{\prime}\Big),

\displaystyle\Phi\big(m:f_{!}\,x\to^{K}_{\langle v,g\rangle}x^{\prime}\big)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\big(m\circ\eta_{x}:x\to_{Q\,b\,e}f_{!}\,x\to^{K}_{\langle v,g\rangle}x^{\prime}\big)

Now,
\Phi
being a fiberwise equivalence means the proposition

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,b^{\prime}:B\\
e:P\,b\\
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}\\
g:e\to^{P}_{v}e^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}d:P\,b\\
f:d\to_{P\,b}e\\
x:Q(b,d)\end{subarray}}\prod_{x^{\prime}:Q(b^{\prime},e^{\prime})}\prod_{k:x\to^{K}_{\langle v,gf\rangle}x^{\prime}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{m:f_{!}\,x\to^{K}_{\langle v,g\rangle}x^{\prime}}m\circ\eta_{x}=k\Big)

is satisfied, cf.
Figure

2
. This exhibits
\eta_{x}:x\to^{K}_{\langle b,f\rangle}\tau_{\varphi}(f,x)
as
K
-cocartesian lift of the
P
-vertical arrow
f:d\to_{P\,b}e
(starting at
x:Q(b,d)
), as claimed.

∎

Figure 3.
Action on arrows of lifting and transport of sliced cocartesian families

Remark 3.6

(Sliced cocartesian families: actions on arrows of the induced functors)
.

For future reference, we record here the actions on arrows of the induced (fibered) lifting and transport functors as established in the proof of
Theorem

3.5
. Informally, these can be described as follows. An arrow in
\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E
is given by a dependent square
\sigma
in
P
whose vertical edges
f\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda t.\sigma(1,t)
,
g^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda t.\sigma(0,t)
are
P
-vertical, and, moreover, an arrow
k
in
Q
over the edge
\langle t,0\rangle
. The lifting functor
\chi_{B}
maps this to the ensuing dependent square in
Q
produced by adding the
Q
-cocartesian lifts of the
P
-vertical arrows and the induced filling edge. The functor
\tau_{B}
yields only the filling edge of this square.

Formally, this reads as

The following proposition reflects the known fact that, given a cocartesian functor between cocartesian fibrations, it is a sliced cocartesian fibration if and only if it is a cocartesian fibration in the usual sense.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type. Assume
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow B
and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
are cocartesian fibrations, and
\varphi:F\to_{B}E
is a cocartesian functor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Then
\varphi:F\to_{B}E
is a cocartesian fibration sliced over
B
if and only if it is a cocartesian fibration
F\twoheadrightarrow E
in the usual sense.

Proof.

In case
\varphi:F\to E
is a cocartesian fibration it is also a cocartesian fibration sliced over
B
since it automatically satisfies the weaker existence condition for lifts.

For the converse, we fibrantly replace the given diagram, considering the straightenings
P\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{B}(\pi):B\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{B}(\pi):B\to\mathcal{U}
,
K\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{E}(\varphi):E\to\mathcal{U}
.

We assume
K
to be a cocartesian family sliced over
B
, and want to show that it is also a cocartesian family in the usual sense. For an illustration of what follows, cf.
Figure

4
. Consider an arrow
\langle u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime},f:e\to_{u}^{\pi}e^{\prime}\rangle
in
E
together with a point
x:K(b,e)\equiv Q(b,e)
. First, consider the
P
-cocartesian lift of
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}
w.r.t.
e:P\,b
, given by
g\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P_{!}(u,e):e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{P}_{u}u^{P}_{!}\,x
. This induces a filler
h\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}^{P}_{g}(f):u^{P}_{!}\,x\to_{P\,b^{\prime}}e^{\prime}
that is in particular vertical. Since
K:E\to\mathcal{U}
is a sliced cocartesian family we have a lift w.r.t. to the
Q
-cocartesian
transport of the point
x:Q(b,e)
, namely an arrow
m\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}K_{!}(h,u_{!}^{Q}\,x):u_{!}^{Q}\,x\to^{K}_{\langle u,f\rangle}x^{\prime}
to some point
x^{\prime}:Q(b,e^{\prime})
. But by assumption,
m
(together with its
\varphi
-image
h
) is also a
Q
-cocartesian arrow, hence so is the composite
k\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}m\circ Q_{!}(u,x):x\longrightarrow^{K}_{g\circ\varphi(Q_{!}(u,x))}x^{\prime}
.
The functor
\varphi
being cocartesian means
\varphi(Q_{!}(u,x))
is identified with
P_{!}(u,e)\equiv g
, so up to homotopy, the dependent arrow
k:x\to^{Q}_{u}x^{\prime}
lies over the composite
h\circ g=f
—hence we can assume it does so strictly. Now,
k
(together with its projection
f
) being a
Q
-cocartesian arrow means it is in particular
K
-cocartesian (cf.
Figure

4
for illustration).
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\equiv Q_{!}(u,x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
