Absolute from sliced cocartesian fibrations

Proposition 3.8
.

Consider a cospan
\psi:F\to_{B}G\leftarrow_{B}E:\varphi
of fibered functors between isoinner fibrations over a Rezk type
B
, giving rise to the sliced comma type:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

The codomain projection

\partial_{1}:\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\psi\twoheadrightarrow F

is a cocartesian fibration.

Proof.

Consider the families
R:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
P:G\to\mathcal{U}
, and
Q:G\to\mathcal{U}
associated to
G\twoheadrightarrow B
,
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow G
, and
\psi:F\twoheadrightarrow G
, resp. By projection equivalence we have

\partial_{1}:\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\psi\simeq\sum_{b:B}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x,x^{\prime}:R\,b}}{{k:x\to^{R}_{u}x^{\prime}}}}P\,b\,x\times Q\,b\,x^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow F\simeq\sum_{b:B}\sum_{x^{\prime}:R\,b}Q\,b\,x^{\prime}.

For an arrow in
F
, given by data
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}
,
m:x^{\prime}\to^{R}_{u}x^{\prime\prime}
,
f:d\to^{Q}_{m}d^{\prime}
, we posit the cocartesian lift w.r.t. the starting vertex
\langle b,k:x\to_{P\,b}x^{\prime},d,e\rangle
to be the “tautological extension”

\langle\langle\id_{x},m\rangle:k\rightrightarrows^{R}_{u}mk,\id_{e}:e=_{P\,x}e,f:d\to^{Q}_{m}d^{\prime}\rangle

as illustrated:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces mk}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

In the picture the right hand side indicates the action of the projection
\partial_{1}:\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\psi\twoheadrightarrow F

That this arrow in the sliced comma
\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\psi
is in fact cocartesian is seen as follows. A postcomposing arrow in
F
consists of data
v:b^{\prime}\to b^{\prime\prime}
,
\ell:x^{\prime\prime}\to_{v}^{R}x^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
g:d^{\prime}\to^{Q}_{\ell}d^{\prime\prime}
. A dependent arrow in
\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\psi
over the composite arrow in
F
is given by

\langle\langle\ell^{\prime},\ell m\rangle:k\rightrightarrows^{R}_{vu}k^{\prime},r:e\to^{P}_{\ell^{\prime}}e^{\prime},g\circ f:d\to^{Q}_{\ell\circ m}d^{\prime\prime}\rangle

where
\ell^{\prime}:x\to_{v}^{R}y
,
k^{\prime}:y\to_{R\,b^{\prime\prime}}x^{\prime\prime\prime}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}\psi}}

The filler is constructed just by repeating the missing data
\ell^{\prime}:x\to y
,
r:e\to e^{\prime}
which also shows uniqueness up to contractibility w.r.t. the given data.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

For a cospan of maps between Rezk types
g:C\to A\leftarrow B:f
, the codomain projection
\partial_{1}:f\mathbin{\downarrow}g\twoheadrightarrow C
is a cocartesian fibration.

3.3.
Cocartesian families in cartesian families

We now introduce the concept of
cocartesian fibrations in cartesian fibrations
, which semantically, really translate to cocartesian fibrations in
\infty
-cosmoses of cartesian fibrations.

Their characterization will be of relevance for the characterization of two-sided cartesian fibrations in
Theorem

4.8
.

Definition 3.10

(Cocartesian fibrations in cartesian fibrations)
.

Let
\varphi:\xi\to_{B}\pi
be a cartesian functor between cartesian fibrations. In addition, let
\varphi
be a
cocartesian
fibration sliced over
B
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Then we call
\varphi
a
cocartesian fibration in cartesian fibrations
.
16
16

16

For the official naming we prefer the fibrational variant since it is closer to its semantic counterpart, but of course by the typal Grothendieck construction there exists an indexed variant as well.

Proposition 3.11

(Char. of cocartesian fibration in cartesian fibrations)
.

Let
\varphi:\xi\to_{B}\pi
be a cocartesian fibration in cartesian fibrations, as in
Definition

3.10
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

The sliced cocartesian lifting map, the fibered LARI

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Vert}_{\xi}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

is a
cartesian functor
between cartesian fibrations over
B

(2)

The sliced cocartesian transport map,
i.e.
, the fibered left adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

is a cartesian functor (from
\overline{\varphi}
to
\xi
).

(3)

For all elements
b,b^{\prime}:B
, arrows
v:b^{\prime}\to_{B}v
, vertical arrows

and
x:Q(b,e^{\prime})
, let us make the following abbreviations:
17
17

17

Note that all the cocartesian lifts exist because they are over
P
-vertical arrows.

(3)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P^{*}(v,e^{\prime}):v^{*}(b^{\prime}e^{\prime})\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

\tau_{B}

Theorems

and
3.6

(12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle v,\langle g,f,g^{\prime},f^{\prime}\rangle,\langle k,m,m^{\prime},k^{\prime}\rangle\rangle

and for the right hand side:

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}^{*}(v,\chi_{B}(f,x))=\overline{\xi}^{*}(v,\langle g,f,g^{\prime},f^{\prime}\rangle,\langle k,m,m^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\rangle)

Recall that a functor being cartesian is a proposition.
A path between (
12
) and (
14
) amounts to an isomorphism
r:(f^{\prime})^{*}f_{!}\,x=_{v^{*}(b,e)}g^{\prime}_{!}g^{*}x
such that the entire following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}\,x}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{\prime})^{*}f_{!}\,x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{!}\,x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g_{!}^{\prime}g^{*}x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime\prime}}

More generally, it can be shown that there exists a filler
r:(f^{\prime})^{*}f_{!}\,x\to_{v^{*}(b,e)}g_{!}^{\prime}g^{*}x
s.t.
m^{\prime\prime}=rm^{\prime}
and
k^{\prime}=k^{\prime\prime}r
. Hence, this propositional condition is equivalent to this induced arrow being invertible. But moreover, we can see by universality that this is equivalent to the existence of either identification
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime\prime}
or
k^{\prime}=k^{\prime\prime}
. In particular, the action by the transport functor
\tau_{B}
yields just the latter. Hence, all the four conditions claimed are equivalent.
∎

Proposition 3.12

(Closure of sliced cocartesian fibrations under product)
.

For a small indexing type
I:\mathcal{U}
, let
B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be a family of Rezk types. Let
P:\prod_{i:I}(B_{i}\to\mathcal{U})
be a family and
K:\prod_{i:I}(\widetilde{P}_{i}\to\mathcal{U})
be another family. We define
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda i.\Sigma_{\widetilde{P_{i}}}K_{i}.\prod_{i:I}(B_{i}\to\mathcal{U})
. For every
i:I
, we denote

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B_{i}}(P_{i}):E_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P_{i}}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i},

\displaystyle\quad\xi_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B_{i}}(Q_{i}):F_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{Q_{i}}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i},

\displaystyle\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{E_{i}}(K_{i}):F_{i}\to E_{i}

giving rise to diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

If each
\varphi_{i}
is a sliced cocartesian fibration, then so is the product:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\xi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}}

Moreover, if
\varphi_{i}
is a cocartesian fibration in cartesian fibrations in the sense of
3.10
, then so is
\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}
.

Proof.

Since dependent products commute with sliced commas by
Proposition

C.1
we find

(15)

\displaystyle\prod_{i:I}{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi_{i}}(F_{i})

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B_{i}}F_{i}\simeq\big(\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\big)\mathbin{\downarrow}_{\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}\big(\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\big)

(16)

\displaystyle\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B_{i}}E_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\big(\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}\big)\mathbin{\downarrow}_{\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}\big(\prod_{i:I}E_{i}\big).

Since sliced LARIs are preserved by the dependent product
Proposition

C.3
we obtain an induced fibered LARI between these commas, establishing that
\prod_{i:I}\varphi
is sliced cocartesian by
Theorem

3.5
.

Moreover, since cartesian fibrations and co-/cartesian functors are preserved under the dependent product the analogous closure statement for the stronger notion of cocartesian fibration in cartesian fibrations follows readily.
∎

Lemma 3.13

(Pullback of fibered cocartesian sections)
.

For a Rezk type
B
, consider cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
, and a fiberwise map
\varphi:\prod_{b:B}P\,b\to Q\,b
. We write the unstraightenings as
\pi:E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}\twoheadrightarrow B
,
\xi:F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{Q}\twoheadrightarrow B
. Consider the following diagram, induced by a section
\ell
of (the totalization of)
\varphi
over
B
, and
A
and a map
k:A\to B
between Rezk types:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

If
\ell
is a cocartesian functor, then the induced section
\ell^{\prime}
is, too.

Proof.

First, projection equivalence yields:

E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b:B}P\,b,\quad F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
e:P\,b\end{subarray}}Q\,b\,e,E^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{a:A}P\,ka,\quad F^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:B\\
d:P\,ka\end{subarray}}Q\,ka\,d.

The section
\ell
is then taken to be

\ell(b,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle b,e,\widehat{\ell}(b,e)\rangle

for
b:B
,
e:P\,b
.
Cocartesianness means that there is a path

\displaystyle\ell(u,P_{!}(u,e))

\displaystyle\equiv\langle u,P_{!}(u,e),\widehat{\ell}(u,P_{!}(u,e))\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\langle u,P_{!}(u,e),Q_{!}(u,\langle u^{P}_{!}e,\widehat{\ell}(b,e)\rangle)\rangle

for
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}
,
e:P\,b
.
The induced section
\ell^{\prime}
arises as
\ell^{\prime}(a,d)\equiv\ell(ka,d)\equiv\langle ka,d\widehat{\ell}(ka,d)\rangle
for
a:A
,
d:P^{\prime}\,a\simeq P\,ka
. Applying this to the
P^{\prime}
-cocartesian lift of
v:a\to a^{\prime}
w.r.t.
d:Q\,a
yields

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(v,P^{\prime}_{!}(v,d))

\displaystyle\equiv\ell(kv,P^{\prime}_{!}(kv,d))=\ell(kv,P_{!}(v,d))\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle kv,P_{!}(v,d),\widehat{\ell}(kv,P_{!}(v,d))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle kv,P_{!}(kv,d),Q_{!}(kv,\langle(kv)^{P}_{!}(d),\widehat{\ell}(kv,P_{!}(kv,d))\rangle)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle kv,P^{\prime}_{!}(v,d),Q^{\prime}_{!}(v,\langle v^{P^{\prime}}_{!}(d),\widehat{\ell^{\prime}}(v,P^{\prime}_{!}(v,d))\rangle)\rangle

confirming the claim.
∎

Proposition 3.14

(Closure of sliced cocartesian fibrations under composition)
.

Let
P,Q,R:B\to\mathcal{U}
be isoinner families over a Rezk type
B
with unstraightenings

\xi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(Q):F\twoheadrightarrow B,\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(P):E\twoheadrightarrow B,\rho\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(R):G\twoheadrightarrow B.

Furthermore, assume we have fibered functors
\varphi:F\to_{B}E
,
\psi:E\to_{B}G
that are sliced cocartesian over
B
. Then, so is their composite
\kappa:F\to_{B}G
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

Moreover, if
\varphi
and
\psi
are cocartesian fibrations in cartesian fibrations, then so is
\psi\circ\varphi
.

Proof.

This proof works analogously to the one for the absolute situation in
[
18
, Proposition 2.3.7]
. First of all, we fibrantly replace the objects at play (with some abbreviation for the term declarations):

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{b:B}Q\,b,

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
x:R\,b\end{subarray}}P\,b\,x,

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
x:R\,b\\
e:P\,b\,x\end{subarray}}Q\,b\,\,x\,e,

\displaystyle\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}G

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{b,x,e}\sum_{x^{\prime}:R\,b}(x\to_{R\,b}x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\varphi\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}G

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{b,x,x^{\prime},e}\sum_{d:Q\,b\,x\,e}(x\to_{R\,b}x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{b,x,x^{\prime},e,d}\sum_{u:x\to_{R\,b}x^{\prime}}(e\to_{u}^{P}e^{\prime}),

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{b,x,x^{\prime},u,e,e^{\prime}}(e\to_{u}^{P}e^{\prime}).

We are to construct from the given fibered LARI adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

a fibered LARI adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Using the projection equivalences, indeed we find the diagram analogous to the proof in
[
18
, Proposition 2.3.7]
, all fibered over
B
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

As before, the proclaimed fibered LARI arises by pulling back and then composing.

The closure property descends to cocartesian fibrations in cartesian fibrations by closedness under composition of cartesian functors, and pullback stability of fibered cartesian sections by the dual of
Lemma

3.13
.
∎

Proposition 3.15

(Closure of sliced cocartesian fibrations under pullback)
.

Let
\varphi:F\to_{B}E
be a sliced cocartesian fibration over a Rezk type
B
. For any map
k:A\to B
consider the pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then the induced fibered functor
k^{*}\varphi:k^{*}F\to_{A}k^{*}E
is a sliced cocartesian fibration over
A
.

In particular, the analogous statement is true if
\varphi
is assumed to be a cocartesian fibration in cartesian fibrations.

Proof.

Since pullback commutes with sliced commas
18
18

18

as can e.g. be checked by projection equivalence
we get the following square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}{\mathrm{Vert}}_{k^{*}\xi}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{A}k^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The fibered LARI on the right gets pulled back to define a fibered LARI on the left, as desired, hence the pulled back functor
k^{*}\varphi
is sliced cocartesian as well.

If
\varphi:F\to_{B}E
is a cartesian functor between cartesian functors, then
\varphi^{\prime}
pulls back to define a cartesian functor between cartesian fibrations by (the dual of)
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.21]
, cf. also
[
68
, Lemma 5.3.5]
. In case the fibered LARI is cartesian, the induced fibered LARI is as well, as one can see by the dual of
Lemma

3.13
.

∎

4.
Two-sided cartesian families

Two-sided cartesian families can be presented by spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\xi
is a cocartesian fibration and
\pi
is a cartesian fibration, satisfying some compatibility conditions between the lifts of opposite variance.

We will develop this notion in a systematic manner, closely following Riehl–Verity’s treatment in
\infty
-cosmoses
[
68
, Chapter 7]
.

Semantically, they correspond to
(\infty,1)
-functors
A^{\Op}\times B\to{\mathrm{Cat}}_{\infty}
, and their
discrete
variant models
(\infty,1)
-functors
(\infty,1)
-functors
A^{\Op}\times B\to\mathrm{Space}
factoring through the
(\infty,1)
-category of spaces
aka

(\infty,1)
-distributors or
(\infty,1)
-profunctors (sometimes also called correspondence or bifibration, though the latter terminology can also be used to mean a fibration that is simultaneously cartesian and cocartesian).

(Discrete) two-sided cartesian fibrations have a long history of study in various contexts
[
100
,
86
,
87
,
60
,
13
,
12
,
47
,
8
,
94
,
54
,
29
,
22
,
55
]
.

In the higher categorical context they are used in the theory of fibrations of
(\infty,1)
-categories
[
49
,
7
,
11
,
35
,
83
]
, the formal category theory of
\infty
-categories
[
68
,
72
,
75
]
, and higher algebra and geometry
[
48
,
34
,
30
]
.

4.1.
Two-variable families and bifibers

For types
A,B:\mathcal{U}
consider a family

P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}.

For
a:A
and
b:B
, the type
P(a,b)
is called the
bifiber
of
P
at
a
and
b
. Fixing one of the elements gives rise to the definitions

P_{b}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda a.P(a,b):A\to\mathcal{U},\quad P^{a}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.P(a,b):B\to\mathcal{U}.

The two “legs” of the family
P
are given by the families

P_{B}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda a.\sum_{b:B}P_{b}(a):A\to\mathcal{U},\quad P^{A}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\sum_{a:B}P^{a}(b):B\to\mathcal{U}.

We obtain the following version of the typal Grothendieck construction. By transposition we have a chain of fiberwise equivalences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{A,B:\mathcal{U}}A\to B\to\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{A,B:\mathcal{U}}(A\times B)\to\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{A,B:\mathcal{U}}\mathrm{Fib}_{\mathcal{U}}(A\times B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Hence two-sided families
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
correspond to maps over
\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:\widetilde{P}\to A\times B
.

Diagrammatically, this manifests as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}P\,a\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{A}(P_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{B}(P^{A})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\pi}

The fibers at
a:A
or
b:B
, resp., are obtained as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\id_{A},b\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a,\id_{B}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where the projections arise as unstraightenings

\xi_{b}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{A}(P_{b}):E_{b}\to A,\quad\pi^{a}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(P^{a}):E^{a}\to B.

The notation comes from the convention of denoting the components of the projection
E\to A\times B
conceived as a fibered functor in two different ways

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

as

\xi_{b}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle_{b}:E_{b}\to(A\times B)_{b}\simeq A,\quad\pi^{a}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle^{a}:E^{a}\to(A\times B)^{a}\simeq B.

4.2.
Cocartesianness on the left

Definition 4.1

(Cocartesian on the left)
.

A two-sided family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
is
cocartesian on the left
if the family
P_{B}:A\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian, and every
P_{B}
-cocartesian arrow in
\widetilde{P}
is
P^{A}
-vertical.

Example 4.2

(Cocartesian families)
.

A family
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian if and only if it is cocartesian on the left, seen as a family
P:(A\to\mathcal{U})\simeq(A\to 1\to\mathcal{U})
.

Proposition 4.3

(Characterizations of cocartesianness on the left,
[
68
, Lemma 7.1.1]
)
.

For a two-sided family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
, corresponding to
\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\to A\times B
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

The fibered functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

is a cocartesian fibration sliced over
B
.

(2)

The fibered functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

is a cocartesian functor between cocartesian fibrations.

(3)

The fibered functor given by

\iota_{\xi}:E\to_{A\times B}\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A,\quad\iota_{\xi}(a,b,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle a,a,\id_{a},b,e\rangle

has a fibered left adjoint
\tau_{\xi}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\pi\circ\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(4)

The two-sided family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian on the left.

Proof.

We abbreviate
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\to_{B}A\times B
.

\ref{it:coc-left-i}\iff\ref{it:coc-left-iv}
:

The fibered functor
\varphi
being a cocartesian fibration sliced over
B
is equivalent to the condition that for all
q
-vertical maps exist a
\varphi
-cocartesian lift (w.r.t. to a given initial vertex). By Rezk-completeness, this is equivalent to any arrow
\langle u:a\to a^{\prime},\id_{b}\rangle
having a
\varphi
-cocartesian lift
f
with prescribed initial vertex
e:P(a,b)
. Note that
f
is
\pi
-vertical. Projecting away the
B
-component, we obtain that
f
is
\xi
-cocartesian.

\ref{it:coc-left-ii}\iff\ref{it:coc-left-iv}
:

Since
p:A\times B\twoheadrightarrow A
is a cocartesian fibration in any case, the assumption is equivalent to
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
being a cocartesian fibration and every
\xi
-cocartesian arrow being mapped to
p
-cocartesian arrows under
\varphi
. But
\varphi
is just the projection pairing
\langle\xi,\pi\rangle
, and
p
-cocartesian arrows are exactly given by arrows whose
B
-component is an identity. Projecting down to
B
this means exactly that the
\xi
-cocartesian arrows are
\pi
-vertical.

\ref{it:coc-left-iii}\implies\ref{it:coc-left-iv}
:

We denote

\tau_{\xi}(a^{\prime},b,u:a\to a^{\prime},e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle a^{\prime},b,\widehat{\tau}_{\xi,a^{\prime},b}(u,e)\rangle.

Again, similarly to the considerations in the proof of
Theorem

2.13
, the unit is a family of arrows

\eta:\prod_{a^{\prime},b:A}\prod_{a:A,u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime}}\prod_{e:P(a,b)}\langle a^{\prime},b,a,u,e\rangle\to\langle a^{\prime},b,a^{\prime},\id_{a^{\prime}},\widehat{\tau}_{\xi,a^{\prime},b}(u,e)\rangle,

illustrated as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\tau}_{\xi,a^{\prime},b}(u,e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{a^{\prime},b,a,u,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{b}}

By assumption, the transposing map induced by
\eta
is an equivalence:

\displaystyle\Phi_{\eta}

\displaystyle:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}:A\\
b,b^{\prime}:B\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\prime}:a^{\prime}\to_{A}a^{\prime\prime}\\
v:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime}\\
e:P(a,b)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime\prime}:A\\
b^{\prime}:B\\
d:P(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\left(\langle a^{\prime},b,\widehat{\tau}_{\xi,a^{\prime},b}(u,e):P(a^{\prime},b)\rangle\to_{\langle u^{\prime},v\rangle}\langle a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime},d\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\left(\langle a^{\prime},b,a,u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime},e:P(a,b)\rangle\to_{\langle u^{\prime},v\rangle}\langle a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},\id_{a^{\prime\prime}},d:P(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime})\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\eta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u^{\prime},v,e,d,g.\iota_{\xi}(g)\circ\eta_{u,e}:\big(e\to_{\langle v,g\rangle}^{P}d\big)

After contracting away redundant data, this is equivalent to the proposition

\prod_{h:e\to^{P}_{\langle u^{\prime}u,v\rangle}d}\isContr\Big(\sum_{g:\widehat{\tau}_{\xi,a^{\prime},b}(u,e)\to^{P}_{\langle u^{\prime},v\rangle}d}g\circ^{P}_{\eta_{u,e}}=h\Big),

cf.
Figure

5
for illustration. But this precisely means that
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
is a cocartesian fibration whose cocartesian lifts all are
\pi
-vertical, namely the components of the fibered unit
\eta
.

\ref{it:coc-left-iv}\implies\ref{it:coc-left-iii}
:

We can strictify the diagram as follows, including the fibered left adjoint to be defined:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime}:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}P(a^{\prime},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,a^{\prime}:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}(a\to_{A}a^{\prime})\times P(a,b)\simeq\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\pi\circ\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The fibered “inclusion” map is defined as

\iota_{\xi}(a^{\prime},b,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle a^{\prime},b,a^{\prime},\id_{a^{\prime}},e\rangle.

By the preconditions from
Item

3
, the map
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
is a cocartesian fibration, moreover whose cocartesian lifts are all
\pi
-vertical. We let

\tau_{\xi}(a^{\prime},b,a,u:a\to a^{\prime},e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle a^{\prime},b,u_{!}\,e\rangle.

Now, similarly, as in the proof of
Theorem

2.13
, we exhibit the fibered adjunction as given by a fiberwise equivalence
19
19

19

Here, we write
\xi_{b}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(\id_{A}\times b)^{*}\langle\xi,\phi\rangle:E_{b}\simeq\sum_{a:A}P(a,b)\twoheadrightarrow A
, giving rise to the comma object
\sum_{a:A}(a\to_{A}a^{\prime})\times P(a,b)

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime}:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime}\\
e:P(a,b)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\prod_{e^{\prime}:P(a^{\prime},b)}\big(\tau_{\xi,a^{\prime},b}(a,u,u_{!}\,e)\to_{P(a^{\prime},b)}e^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\big(\langle a,u,e\rangle\longrightarrow_{\xi_{b}\mathbin{\downarrow}A}\langle a^{\prime},\id_{a^{\prime}},e^{\prime}\rangle\big)

as follows: Over a point
\langle a^{\prime},b\rangle:A\times B
in the base, fix
\langle a:A,u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime},e:P(a,b)\rangle
,
e^{\prime}:P(a^{\prime},b)
, and define maps between the transposing hom types, in opposite directions,

\displaystyle\Phi(g:u_{!}e\to_{P(a^{\prime},b)}e^{\prime})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}g\circ\xi_{!}(u,e),

\displaystyle\Psi(u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime},u:u\to_{A\mathbin{\downarrow}a}\id_{a^{\prime}},h:e\to_{u}^{P}e^{\prime})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{\xi_{!}(u,e)}^{\xi}(h).

Again, by the universal property of cocartesian lifts it can be checked that the two maps are quasi-inverses. Note that by assumption, the
\xi
-cocartesian lifts are
\pi
-vertical, so everything stays in “the fibers over
b
”, or, more precisely, in the pullback type
(\id_{A}\times b)^{*}E
.

Lifts in a family that is cocartesian on the left

∎

By dualization, one obtains the notion of a two-sided family
cartesian on the right
. As a corollary, we obtain a characterization of the conjunction of both properties.

Corollary 4.4

(
[
68
, Corollary 7.1.3]
)
.

A two-variable family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right if and only if the following equivalent propositions are true.

(1)

The fibered functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

is both a cartesian functor between cartesian fibrations and a relative cocartesian fibration over
B
.

(2)

The fibered functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

is both a cocartesian functor between cocartesian fibrations and a relative cartesian fibration over
B
.

is cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right, we denote the ensuing lifting operations as follows. Given

two-sided cartesian fibration
adds on top a certain compatibility condition between the lifts of different variance. Before defining two-sided cartesian fibrations in

, we first investigate the compatibility condition in the following lemma.

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{f}^{\xi}(k^{\prime}k):u_{!}\,v^{*}\,e\to_{\langle a^{\prime},v\rangle}^{\xi}u_{!}\,e

\displaystyle g^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{f^{\prime}}^{\pi}(k^{\prime}k):v^{*}\,e\to^{\pi}_{\langle u,b^{\prime}\rangle}v^{*}\,u_{!}\,e

We claim that there is an identification between the following two induced morphisms
h,h^{\prime}:u_{!}\,v^{*}\,e\to_{P(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}v^{*}\,u_{!}\,e
(cf.
7
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}u_{!}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}u_{!}\,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h:\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}^{\pi}_{f^{\prime}}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}:\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}^{\xi}_{f}(g^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

Proof.

It is sufficient to provide an identification
g=f^{\prime}h^{\prime}
. For this, it is sufficient—and necessary—to provide a witness for
f^{\prime}(h^{\prime}f)=k^{\prime}k
. But this follows from
f^{\prime}h=g
, since
gf=k^{\prime}k
.
∎

Comparing cartesian and cocartesian transport

4.3.
Two-sided cartesian families

4.3.1.
Definition and examples of two-sided cartesian fibrations

Definition 4.6

(Two-sided cartesian families,
[
68
, Section 7.1]
)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a (two-sided) family, where
A
and
B
are Rezk types. We call
P
a
two-sided cartesian family
(short:
two-sided family
) if

(1)

P
is cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right,

(2)

and
P
satisfies the condition that
cocartesian and cartesian lifts commute
: for any
a:A
,
b:B
,
e:P\,a\,b
and arrows
u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime}
,
v:b^{\prime}\to_{B}b
, the filler
\kappa:u_{!}(v^{*}e)\to v^{*}(u_{!}e)
from
Lemma

4.5
is an isomorphism, hence there is an identity
u_{!}v^{*}e=_{P(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}v^{*}u_{!}e
.

For any Rezk type
A
the
\hom
-span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}

is a two-sided cartesian fibration: the
\partial_{1}
-cocartesian lift are exactly the
\partial_{0}
-vertical squares, and vice versa, and the transports satisfy the necessary compatibility condition (cf.
[
18
, Proposition 5.2.15 and Corollary 5.2.16]
). In fact, this gives an example of a
discrete
two-sided family, since all the fibers are discrete types, cf.
[
66
, Proposition 8.29]
, as well as
Definition

7.1
and
Proposition

7.2
.

More generally, comma fibrations of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\mathbin{\downarrow}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}

for functors
f:B\to A
and
g:C\to A
yield examples of two-sided discrete cartesian fibrations, by the analogous arguments.

Furthermore, given two-sided cartesian fibrations
E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
and
F\twoheadrightarrow B\times C
, we will see that their
span composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times_{B}Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is a two-sided cartesian fibration, too. For example, composing the
\hom
-span
A^{\Delta^{1}}\twoheadrightarrow A\times A
, which is a discrete two-sided fibration, with itself yields a two-sided cartesian fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

whose total type is equivalent to
A^{\Delta^{2}}
due to
A
being Segal. Its fiber at
\langle c,a\rangle:A\times A
is
\sum_{b:A}\hom(a,b)\times\hom(b,c)
, which is in general not discrete. Thus,
A^{\Delta^{2}}\twoheadrightarrow A\times A
is an example of a not necessarily discrete two-sided cartesian fibration.
20
20

20

We are grateful to Emily Riehl for pointing out this example.

Furthermore, any family
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
with associated span
\xi:A\leftarrow E\rightarrow B:\pi
of functors gives rise to the
free-two sided fibration
, see
[
81
]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{\mathrm{2s}}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

That this is a two-sided cartesian fibration satisfying the corresponding universal property can be proven using the results about the free cocartesian fibration from
[
18
, Subsection 5.2.17]
and their dual analogue for the free cartesian fibration.

The fiber of
L^{\mathrm{2s}}(E)\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
at
\langle a,b\rangle:A\times B
is given, up to equivalence, by the type
\sum_{a^{\prime}:A}\sum_{b^{\prime}:B}(a^{\prime}\to_{A}a)\times(b\to_{B}b^{\prime})\times P(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
.

4.3.2.
Characterization of two-sided cartesian families

Proposition 4.7

(Commutation of cocartesian and cartesian lifts)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a family with both
A
and
B
Rezk which is cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right. We denote by
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow A
and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, resp., the unstraightenings.
Then cocartesian and cartesian lifts commute if and only if the following property is satisfied:
Given
u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime}
,
v:b^{\prime}\to_{B}b
,
e:P\,a\,b
, and a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\pi^{*}(v,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\xi_{!}(u,e)}

where
g
(and necessarily
k^{\prime}
) is
\xi
-vertical and
f
(and necessarily
k
) is
\pi
-vertical. Then
f
is
\xi
-cocartesian if and only if
g
is
\pi
-cartesian.

Proof.

In light of
Lemma

4.5
, the commutation condition is equivalent to the type of paths
h:u_{!}\,v^{*}\,e=_{P(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}v^{*}\,u_{!}\,e
together with witnesses, necessarily propositional, that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,u_{!}\,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\xi_{!}(u,v^{*}\,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{f}^{\xi}(kk^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\pi^{*}(v,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\xi_{!}(u,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{f^{\prime}}^{\pi}(kk^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\pi^{*}(v,u_{!}\,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

But since these diagrams commute in any case by the assumptions

(recall
Lemma

4.5
) said type is equivalent to the proposition that the filler
h
is an isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,u_{!}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Finally due to the commutation of both of the “completed squares” above, this is equivalent to the new alternative criterion: up to identification,
f
is
\xi
-cocartesian if and only if
g
is
\pi
-cartesian.
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Vert}_{\pi}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times_{A\times B}\mathrlap{\mathrm{Vert}_{q}(A\times B)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}\,e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}(v,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fill}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}(v,e^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}(v,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

Figure 8.
Cartesian lifts in the induced fibrations
\pi^{\prime}
and
\psi

The following theorem finally contains several characterizations of a two-variable family being two-sided cartesian.
21
21

21

We are indebted to Emily Riehl for helpful explanations and discussions about
[
68
, Thm. 7.1.4]
.
This consists in several sliced Chevalley/fibered adjoint criteria and a criterion formulated directly on the level of two-variable families.

Theorem 4.8

(Char. of two-sided families,
[
68
, Thm. 7.1.4]
)
.

For a family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
, corresponding to
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{a:A,b:B}P(a,b)\to A\times B
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

The two-variable family
P
is two-sided.

(2)

Considering

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

the map
\pi

is a cartesian fibration, the functor
\varphi
is cartesian and a
cocartesian
fibration sliced over
B
. Furthermore, the fibered LARI

\chi_{B}:{\mathrm{Vert}}_{q}(A\times B)\times_{A\times B}E\to_{B}{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)

is a
cartesian
functor.

(3)

Considering

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

the map
\pi

is a cartesian fibration, the functor
\varphi
is cartesian and a
cocartesian
fibration sliced over
B
. Furthermore, the fibered LARI

\tau_{B}:{\mathrm{Vert}}_{q}(A\times B)\times_{A\times B}E\to_{B}E

is a
cartesian
functor.

(4)

Considering

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

the map
\xi

is a cocartesian fibration, the functor
\varphi
is cocartesian and a
cartesian
fibration sliced over
A
. Furthermore, the fibered RARI

\chi^{A}:{\mathrm{Vert}}_{p}(A\times B)\times_{A\times B}E\to_{A}{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi}(E)

is a
cocartesian
functor.

(5)

The fibered adjoints in the following diagram exist:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\downarrow\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A\times_{E}B\downarrow\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\iota_{\xi}\circ\partial_{1}^{\prime},\id\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\pi\,\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi\,\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\id,\iota^{\pi}\circ\partial_{0}^{\prime}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where the pullback type is given by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times_{E}B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Moreover, the mate of the identity
2
-cell defines a fibered isomorphism

\prod_{a:A,b:B}(\tau_{\xi}\circ r)_{a,b}=_{Q(a,b)\to P(a,b)}(\tau^{\pi}\circ\ell)_{a,b},

where

Q:A\times B\to\mathcal{U},\quad Q(a,b)\simeq a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\times P(a,b)

is the straightening of the map

F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times_{E}B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi\twoheadrightarrow A\times B.

Proof.

\ref{it:char-two-sid-i}\iff\ref{it:char-two-sid-ii}
:

By
Corollary

4.4
, the map
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right if and only if
\varphi:E\to_{B}A\times B
is both a cartesian functor between cartesian fibrations and a cocartesian fibration sliced over
B
.

In the following, we assume this is satisfied for
P
.

Thus, we are left to show that, under this assumption—
P
being cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right—the following holds:

“The fibered LARI
\chi_{B}:{\mathrm{Vert}}_{q}(A\times B)\times_{A\times B}E\to_{B}{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)

is a cocartesian functor.”

\displaystyle\iff\text{``In $P$, cocartesian and cartesian lifts commute.''}

We write
F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{Vert}_{q}(A\times B)\times_{A\times B}E\twoheadrightarrow B
, so by projection equivalence, we consider the projection

\psi:\sum_{b:B}\sum_{a:A}(a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times P(a,b))\simeq F\twoheadrightarrow B.

The induced sliced Leibniz cotensor is given by

\kappa:{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)\to_{B}F,\quad\kappa_{b}\big(u:\Delta^{1}\to A,f:\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}P(u(t),b)\big)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\partial_{0}\,u,u,\partial_{0}\,f\rangle.

It has a fibered LARI
\mu:F\to_{B}{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)
as indicated in:

it suffices to show that the
\pi
-cartesian arrow
\ell^{\prime}
equalizes both composite arrows. Note that from a path

we obtain a chain of homotopies

(\ell^{\prime}k)g=(f\ell)g=fh=h^{\prime}f^{\prime}=(\ell^{\prime}g^{\prime})f^{\prime}

as desired. Hence the whole diagram
(?)
commutes.

Now, the fibered transport functor

\psi

\pi^{\prime}
-cartesian arrows. Its action on

\displaystyle\mu_{v}(\psi^{*}(v,\langle u,e\rangle))=\mu_{v}\big(\langle\id_{a},\id_{a^{\prime}}\rangle:u\rightrightarrows_{A}u,\pi^{*}(v,e)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\langle\id_{a},\id_{a^{\prime}}\rangle:u\rightrightarrows_{A}u,

\displaystyle\big\langle\pi^{*}(v,e),\mathrm{fill}^{\xi}_{\xi_{!}(u,v^{*}e)}(\xi_{!}(u,e)\circ\pi^{*}(v,e))\big\rangle:\xi_{!}(u,v^{*}e)\rightrightarrows^{P}\xi_{!}(u,e)\Big\rangle,

Cartesianness of fibered lifting functor

\ref{it:char-two-sid-ii}\iff\ref{it:char-two-sid-iii'}
:

This follows from the characterization

of cocartesian families in cartesian families, namely the equivalence of the conditions from

and

.

\ref{it:char-two-sid-i}\iff\ref{it:char-two-sid-iii}

This is dual to the previous case.

\ref{it:char-two-sid-ii}\iff\ref{it:char-two-sid-iv}
:

We adapt the proof of
[
68
, Theorem 7.1.4]
.
First, observe that we have

\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times_{E}B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\times P(a,b).

Now, the assumption of
\varphi
being cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right is equivalent to the existence of the fibered adjoints
\tau_{\xi}
and
\tau^{\pi}
. We will only write down the steps starting with
\tau_{\xi}
, since the case of
\tau^{\pi}
is dual. By pulling back the fibered LARI adjunction
\tau_{\xi}\dashv_{A\times B}\iota_{\xi}
, we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\downarrow\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A\times_{E}B\downarrow\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda u,v,e.\langle u1,v\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\pi\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}\times\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda a^{\prime},v,e.\langle a^{\prime},v,e\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\tau_{\xi}\circ\partial_{1},\id\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

with projection equivalences

\displaystyle\xi\downarrow A

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime}:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}\sum_{u:A\downarrow a^{\prime}}P(u0,b),

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime}:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}P(a^{\prime},b),

\displaystyle A\times B^{\Delta^{1}}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime}:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}B\downarrow b,

\displaystyle B\downarrow\pi

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime}:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}B\downarrow b\times P(a^{\prime},b).

Postcomposition with
\id_{A}:A\times B^{\Delta^{1}}\to A\times B
preserves the fibered adjunction, yielding as desired:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\downarrow\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A\times_{E}B\downarrow\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\tau^{\xi}\circ\partial_{1},\id\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}\times\partial_{0}}

This, together with the dual case, yields the claimed adjoints in the fibered square of

5
.

In sum, this is equivalent to
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
being a cartesian fibration, the fibered functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

being a cartesian functor and a cocartesian fibration sliced over

. The invertibility of the mate is then equivalent to the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

being cartesian.

By
Theorem

2.15
, this is equivalent to the mate of the identity of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times_{E}B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi}}

being invertible, as

B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi\partial_{0}\simeq\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times_{E}B\mathbin{\downarrow}\pi.

∎

5.
Two-sided cartesian functors and closure properties

Definition 5.1

(Two-sided cartesian functors,
[
68
, Prop. 7.1.7]
)
.

Let
P,Q:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be two-sided cartesian families. A fibered map
h:P\to_{A\times B}Q
is called
two-sided cartesian functor
(or simply
cartesian
) if it constitutes a cocartesian functor
h:P_{B}\to_{A}Q_{B}
and a cartesian functor
h:P_{A}\to_{B}Q_{A}
.

An immediate reformulation is that, for all
u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime}
and
v:b^{\prime}\to_{B}v
,
e,d:P(a,b)
, we have identities
22
22

22

In particular, again the types of each of these identities is a proposition, hence so is their product.

h_{u,b}(P_{!}(u,b,e))=Q_{!}(u,b,h_{a,b}(e)),\quad h_{a,v}(P^{*}(a,v,e))=Q^{*}(a,v,h_{a,b}(e)).

We will state versions of the closure properties w.r.t. to different bases as well as the sliced or relative versions where the base stays fixed throughout. All the following subsections are oriented along
[
68
, Section 7.2]
.

5.1.
Composition and whiskering

Proposition 5.2

(Composition stability of two-sided cartesian functors)
.

Let
A,B,C,D,S
, and
T
be Rezk types. Assume given two-sided cartesian families
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:C\to D\to\mathcal{U}
, and
R:S\to T\to\mathcal{U}
, as well as two-sided cartesian functors
h:P\to_{A\times B,C\times D}Q
,
k:Q\to_{C\times D,S\times T}R
. Then the composite fibered functor
k\circ h:P\to_{A\times B,S\times T}R
is a two-sided cartesian functor as well.

Proof.

This follows since cartesian and cocartesian functors are both closed under composition, cf.
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.6, Item 1]
.
∎

Corollary 5.3

(Composition stability of two-sided cartesian functors in a slice)
.

Let
P,Q,R:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be two-sided cartesian families, and
h:P\to_{A\times B}Q
,
k:Q\to_{A\times B}R
cartesian functors. Then the composite fibered functor
k\circ h:P\to_{A\times B}R
is a two-sided cartesian functor as well.

Proposition 5.4

(Whiskering with co-/cart. fibrations
[
68
, Lem. 7.2.5]
)
.

Let
A,B,C,D
be Rezk types. Assume
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
is a two-sided cartesian fibration. If
k:A\twoheadrightarrow C
is a cocartesian fibration and
m:B\twoheadrightarrow D
is a cartesian fibration, then the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\pi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times m}

is a two-sided cartesian fibration as well.

Proof.

We argue as in
[
68
, Lemma 7.2.5]
. By the characterization of two-sided cartesian fibrations via cocartesian fibrations in cartesian fibrations
Theorem

4.8
,
Item

2
, we reason as follows. Since
k:A\twoheadrightarrow C
and
\id_{B}:B\twoheadrightarrow B
both are two-sided cartesian fibrations as well also their cartesian product is, by
Proposition

5.8
. Hence, the following fibered maps are cocartesian fibrations in cartesian fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times\id_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

and so is their horizontal composite
(k\times\id_{B})\circ\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow_{B}C\times B
.

One can argue similarly for the case
\id_{C}:C\twoheadrightarrow C
and
m:B\twoheadrightarrow D
, which establishes the claim.
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall\,u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{b}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{c}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall\,v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall\,r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall\,h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

Figure 11.
Cocartesian lift and universal property in the span composite

Proposition 5.5

(Span composition of two-sided cartesian fibrations, cf.
[
68
, Proposition 7.2.6]
)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:B\to C\to\mathcal{U}
be two-sided cartesian families over Rezk types
A
,
B
,
C
. Then the family defined by
span composition

Q\boxdot P\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda a,c.\sum_{b:B}P\,a\,b\times Q\,b\,c:A\to C\to\mathcal{U}

is also two-sided cartesian.

In particular, the cocartesian and cartesian lifts, resp., are given as follows: For
u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime}
,
w:c^{\prime}\to_{C}c
,
b:B
,
e:P(a,b)
, and
d:Q(b,c)
we have

\displaystyle(Q\boxdot P)_{!}(u:a\to_{A}a^{\prime},\id_{c},\langle b,e,d\rangle)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle u,\id_{c},\id_{b},P_{!}(u,b,e),\id_{d}\rangle,

\displaystyle(Q\boxdot P)^{*}(\id_{a},w,\langle b,e,d\rangle)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\id_{a},w,\id_{b},\id_{e},Q^{*}(b,w,d)\rangle.

Proof.

We can argue on the level of fibrations just as in
[
68
, Proposition 7.2.6]
. Let
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{A,B}(P):E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
,
\psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\kappa,\mu\rangle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B,C}(Q):E\twoheadrightarrow B\times C
. The unstraightening of
Q\boxdot P
corresponds to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times_{B}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q,p\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi,\mu\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\boxdot\varphi}

where
q:E\times_{B}F\to E
and
p:E\times_{B}F\to F
are the projections from the pullback object. Now, the map
\psi\boxdot\varphi:A\to C\to\mathcal{U}
is constructed by first taking the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times_{B}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}\times\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\xi\circ q,p\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and then postcomposing the map on
\langle\xi q,p\rangle:{E\times_{B}F}\twoheadrightarrow A\times F
with
\id_{A}\times\mu:A\times F\to A\times C
. Pullback along products of maps preserves two-sided cartesian fibrations by
Proposition

5.6
, and so does postcomposition with the cartesian product of a cocartesian and a cartesian fibration by
Proposition

5.4
. Hence, the resulting map
\psi\boxdot\varphi:E\times_{B}F\twoheadrightarrow A\times C
is two-sided cartesian as well.

The proclaimed description of the co-/cartesian lifts comes out of this construction, using the descriptions of the lifts from the constructions in
[
18
, Subsections 3.2.4 and 5.3.3]
. Alternatively, one can verify the universal property directly, cf.
Figure

11
. E.g., for the cocartesian case, given any
u^{\prime}:a^{\prime}\to_{A}a^{\prime\prime}
,
w:c\to_{C}c^{\prime}
, an arrow lying over the (component-wise) composite with domain
\langle b,e,d\rangle
consists of some arrow
v:b\to b^{\prime}
and dependent arrows
f:e\to^{P}_{\langle u^{\prime}u,v\rangle}e^{\prime}
,
r:d\to^{Q}_{\langle v,w\rangle}d^{\prime}
. By initiality,
v:\id_{b}\to v
is the unique filler in the comma object
b\mathbin{\downarrow}B
, and so is
r:\id_{d}\to r
, lying over
v:\id_{b}\to v
and
w:\id_{c}\to c^{\prime}
. By cocartesianness, we also find w.r.t. the data given the unique filler
g\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{cocartFill}_{P_{*}(u,b,e)}(f)
with
g\circ f=h
, for
f\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P_{*}(u,b,e):e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}

}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
