\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{P}_{\langle u,b\rangle}u_{*}\,e
in
P
as desired.
∎

5.2.
Pullback and reindexing

Proposition 5.6

(Pullback stability of two-sided cartesian families, cf.
[
68
, Proposition 7.2.4]
)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided cartesian family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. Then for any pair of maps
k:C\to A
,
m:D\to B
, the pullback family

(k\times m)^{*}P:C\to D\to\mathcal{U}

is two-sided as well.
Diagrammatically, if the two-sided fibration
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
denotes the unstraightening of
P
, this means that the map
\psi
in the following diagram is a two-sided fibration:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k\times m)^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k\times m)^{*}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Furthermore, we claim that this square is a two-sided cartesian functor.

Recalling the notation from
Section

4.1
, we write

\displaystyle P_{m}:A\to\mathcal{U},

\displaystyle P_{m}(a)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}}P_{m\,b^{\prime}}(a)\equiv\sum_{b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}}P(a,m\,b^{\prime}),

\displaystyle P^{k}:B\to\mathcal{U},

\displaystyle P^{k}(b)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{a^{\prime}:A^{\prime}}P^{k\,a^{\prime}}(b)\equiv\sum_{a^{\prime}:A^{\prime}}P(k\,a^{\prime},b),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{m^{*}\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times\id_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{B}(P^{k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{A}(P_{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}\times m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In particular, for the case of
k=\id_{A}:A\to A
and
m=\id_{B}:B\to B
we have
P^{A}=P^{\id_{A}}
and
P_{B}=P_{\id_{B}}
, cf.
Section

4.1
.

Proof.

This follows by employing the characterization
Theorem

4.8
,
Item

4
, and then the closure property
Proposition

3.15
. In particular, letting either of the maps
k,m
be an identity, we can conclude that
P^{k}
is cocartesian, and
P_{m}
is cartesian.

That the square is a two-sided cartesian functor follows by separately projecting to the factors in the base, and then using
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.9]
or its dual. Namely, e.g. since
P_{m}:A\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian, so is its pullback along
k:A^{\prime}\to A
which arises as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k\times m)^{*}\widetilde{P}\simeq k^{*}\widetilde{P_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and the pullback square is known to be a cocartesian functor.
∎

Proposition 5.7

(Pullback stability of two-sided cartesian functors)
.

In the following, let all types be Rezk. Consider two-sided cartesian families
P,Q:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
with unstraightenings
E\twoheadrightarrow\to A\times B
of
P
, and
F\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
of
Q
, resp. Let
\kappa:P\to_{A\times B}Q
be a two-sided cartesian functor. Given maps
k:A^{\prime}\to A
,
m:B^{\prime}\to B
, then the functor
\kappa^{\prime}:P^{\prime}\to_{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
induced by pullback along
k\times m
is two-sided cartesian as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proof.

Let
u^{\prime}:a_{0}^{\prime}\to_{A^{\prime}}a_{1}^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}
,
e:P^{\prime}(a_{0}^{\prime},b^{\prime})\simeq P(k\,a_{0}^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime})
. Straightforward calculation gives

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{u^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(P_{!}^{\prime}(u^{\prime},b^{\prime},e))=\kappa_{k\,u^{\prime},m\,^{\prime}}(P_{!}(k\,u^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime},e))

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(k\,u^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime},\kappa_{k\,u^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime}}(e))=Q_{!}^{\prime}(u^{\prime},b^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}_{u^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(e)),

where the second identity is given by
\kappa
being cocartesian. The dual case for cartesian lifts works similarly (cf. also
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.18]
).
∎

5.3.
Dependent and sliced product

Proposition 5.8

(Product stability of two-sided cartesian families)
.

Let
A,B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be families of Rezk types for a small type
I
. Consider a two-sided family
P:\prod_{i:I}A_{i}\to B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
. Then the induced product family

\prod_{i:I}P_{i}:\prod_{i:I}A_{i}\to\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}

is two-sided cartesian as well.

Moreover, denoting the unstraightenings of the
P_{i}
by
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}\times B_{i}
, the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}A_{i}\times\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{k}\times B_{k}}}

are two-sided cartesian functors. Furthermore, these product cones are terminal w.r.t. two-sided cartesian functors.
23
23

23

Here, and in the following we will not formally spell out the universal properties, but they are analogous to the respective propositions in
[
18
, Subsection 5.3.3]
. The addition/generalization is that the base types are binary products, and the fibrations and functors are
two-sided
cartesian.

Fibrationally, the proposition says that given a family of two-sided fibrations
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}\times B_{i}
for
i:I
, the product fibration
\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}:\prod_{i:I} E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{i:I} A_{i}\times\prod_{i:I} B_{i}
is also two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the characterization
Theorem

4.8
,
Item

4
, in combination with the closure property
Proposition

3.12
. Two-sided cartesianness of the projection squares follows upon postcomposition with the respective projection, and then employing either
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.7]
or its dual. Similarly, one argues for the universal property for two-sided cartesian functors, using
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.8]
or its dual, resp.
∎

Corollary 5.9

(Sliced product stability of two-sided cartesian families)
.

Let
A,B
be small Rezk types. Consider a two-sided family
P:\prod_{i:I}A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
. Then the induced fiberweise product family

\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}P_{i}:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}

is two-sided cartesian as well.

Moreover, for every
k:I
there is an induced canonical commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

which is a two-sided functor. The two-sided fibration
\prod_{i:I}E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
is the terminal cone over the
\varphi_{k}:E_{k}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
w.r.t. (triangle-shaped) cones into the
\varphi_{k}
whose horizontal map is two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

Recall that we have equivalences

\displaystyle\prod_{i}E_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:I\to A\\
\beta:I\to B\end{subarray}}\prod_{i:I}P_{i}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\twoheadrightarrow A^{I}\times B^{I},

\displaystyle\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}\prod_{i:I}P_{i}(a,b)\twoheadrightarrow A\times B.

Denote by
E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
the unstraightening of the family
P_{i}
. By
Proposition

5.6
, the induced map
\prod_{i:I}E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow(A\times B)^{I}
is two-sided cartesian:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\times B)^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Invoking pullback-stability, and then considering the straightening of this map to recover a type family establishes the claim.

Now, by the above description via projection equivalence, we have evaluation maps yielding the desired cones
\mathrm{ev}_{k}:{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}\to_{A\times B}E_{k}
for
k:I
. But by the universal property of the standard dependent product
Proposition

5.8
(cf.
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.8]
), these factor as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{I}\times B^{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where the upper horizontal induced functor is two-sided cartesian, as are the evaluations from the standard dependent product. Hence, so is their composite, as desired, by
Proposition

5.2
.
∎

Proposition 5.10

(Pullback cones are two-sided cartesian functors)
.

Consider two-sided families over Rezk types

P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U},\quad P^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}\to\mathcal{U},\quad P:A^{\prime\prime}\to B^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{U}.

Furthermore, assume there are maps

\alpha:A^{\prime}\to A,\quad\alpha^{\prime\prime}:A^{\prime\prime}\to A,\quad\beta^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\to B,\quad\beta^{\prime\prime}:B^{\prime\prime}\to B

and two-sided cartesian functors

\kappa^{\prime}:P^{\prime}\to_{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime},A\times B}P,\quad\kappa^{\prime\prime}:P^{\prime\prime}\to_{A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime},A\times B}P.

Denote by

\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B,\quad\varphi^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime},\quad\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}

the unstraightenings of
P
,
P^{\prime}
, and
P^{\prime\prime}
, resp. Consider the induced pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime})\times_{A\times B}(A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}\times_{\varphi}\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime\prime}\times\beta^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}\times\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}

Then the mediating map

\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\varphi^{\prime}\times_{\varphi}\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime\prime}

where

A^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}A^{\prime}\times_{A}A^{\prime\prime},\quad B^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}B^{\prime}\times_{B}B^{\prime\prime}

is a two-sided cartesian fibration.
24
24

24

Note in particular that we have an equivalence
A^{\prime\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime\prime}\simeq(A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime})\times_{A\times B}(A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime})

Moreover, each of the projection squares from
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a two-sided cartesian functor, and
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime\prime}
satisfies the expected terminal universal property for cones which are two-sided cartesian functors (analogous to
[
18
, Propositions 5.3.10,11]
).

Proof.

We use projection equivalence so that we can take the fibers of
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
to be
25
25

25

Where
a:A
,
a^{\prime}:A^{\prime}
lies strictly over
a
via
k^{\prime}

etc
.

P^{\prime\prime\prime}(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\equiv P^{\prime}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\times_{P(a,b)}P^{\prime\prime}(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}).

We claim that the cocartesian lifts in
P^{\prime\prime\prime}
are then given by

P_{!}^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime},b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime},\langle e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\rangle)\equiv\langle P_{!}(u,b,e),P_{!}^{\prime}(u^{\prime},b^{\prime},e^{\prime}),P_{!}^{\prime\prime}(u^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\rangle,

which can be checked to be cocartesian since the conditions are validated fiberwise. In particular, the cocartesian lifts in
P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime\prime}
indeed lie over the ones in
P
by two-sided cartesian-ness of
\kappa^{\prime}
and
\kappa^{\prime\prime}
. So far, this is analogous to
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.10]
, but we have the additional triple of points
\langle b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\rangle
as data.

The argument for the cartesian lifts works dually.
Now, the compatibility condition from
Proposition

4.7
have to be checked. But by the projection equivalence above, the ensuing proposition is just witnessing that the condition is satisfied component-wise for triples
\langle\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}\rangle
where
\sigma
is a square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

and
\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}
are of the same shape, lying above. Since the compatbility condition is satisfied for each of those, we are done. This shows that
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a two-sided cartesian fibration, as desired.

From the discussion of the lifts, it is also clear that both the projection squares are two-sided cartesian functors, since we just project to the respective coordinates. Furthermore, the universal property is established, again, by postcomposing separately with the projections to either
A^{\prime\prime\prime}
or
B^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then applying either
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.11]
for the one-sided cocartesian case, or its dual for the cartesian case.
∎

Corollary 5.11

(Pullback cones in a slice are two-sided cartesian functors)
.

Consider two-sided families over Rezk types
P,P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
with unstraightenings
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
,
\varphi^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}
, and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
. Given two-sided cartesian functors
\kappa^{\prime}:P^{\prime}\to_{A\times B}P
and
\kappa^{\prime\prime}:P^{\prime\prime}\to P
,
consider the induced pullback over
A\times B
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}

Then the mediating map

\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\varphi^{\prime}\times_{\varphi}\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B

is a two-sided cartesian fibration.

Moreover, each of the projection squares from
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a two-sided cartesian functor, and
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\to A\times B
satisfies the expected terminal universal property for cones which are two-sided cartesian functors over
A\times B
.

5.4.
Sequential limit

Proposition 5.12

(Sequential limit cones are cocartesian functors)
.

Consider an inverse diagram of two-sided cartesian fibrations as below where all of the connecting squares are two-sided cartesian functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{\infty}\times B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}\times B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}\times B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}\times B_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{3},\beta_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}

Then the induced map
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to B_{\infty}
between the limit types is a two-sided cartesian fibration, and the projection squares constitute two-sided cartesian functors.

Furthermore,
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to B_{\infty}
together with the projection squares satisfies the universal property of a sequential limit w.r.t. to cones of two-sided cartesian functors.

Proof.

Using the closure properties from this chapter, we can argue along the lines of
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.12]
.
26
26

26

We thank Ulrik Buchholtz for initially suggesting this proof in
[
18
]
because it circumvents dealing with unwieldy coherence data that would occur in different presentations of the sequential limit.
This means, the limit fibration, again is constructed via the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{\infty}\times B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{2n}}\times\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{2n+1}\times\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}}\times\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and is two-sided cartesian due to
Proposition

5.10
. From
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.12]
and its dual we get that the projection squares are two-sided cartesian functors. The universal property is established using
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.13]
and its dual.
∎

Corollary 5.13

(Sequential limit cones in a slice are cocartesian functors)
.

Consider an inverse diagram of two-sided cartesian fibrations as below where all of the connecting squares are two-sided cartesian functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}

Then the induced map
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to A\times B
between the limit types is a two-sided cartesian fibration, and the projection squares constitute two-sided cartesian functors.

Furthermore,
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
together with the projection squares satisfies the universal property of a sequential limit, relativized to the basis
A\times B
.

Proof.

The sequential limit of a diagram of identity maps is the object itself, e.g.

\seqlim_{n}\langle B,\id_{B}\rangle\simeq\sum_{\sigma:\mathbb{N}\to B}\prod_{n,k:\mathbb{N}}\sigma(n)=\sigma(n+k)\simeq B.

Thus, the claim follows from
Proposition

5.12
.
∎

5.5.
Cotensors

Proposition 5.14

(Cocart. fibrations are cotensored over maps/shape inclusions)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided cartesian family with associated projection
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
. For any type map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
, the maps
\varphi^{X}
and
\varphi^{Y}
are two-sided cartesian fibrations, and moreover the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}\times B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{Y}}

is a two-sided cartesian functor.

Proof.

By closedness under products, the maps
\varphi^{X}
,
\varphi^{Y}
are two-sided cartesian fibrations. From
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.15]
, we know that the square formed by the composites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}\times B^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{Y}}

is a cocartesian functor (since by precondition
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
is a cocartesian fibration). The cartesian case over
B
works the same.
∎

Corollary 5.15

(Cocartesian fibrations in a slice are cotensored over maps/shape inclusions)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided cartesian family with associated projection
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
. For any type map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
, the maps
X{\multimap}\varphi
and
Y{\multimap}\varphi
are two-sided cartesian fibrations, and moreover the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X{\multimap}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y{\multimap}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j{\multimap}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X{\multimap}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y{\multimap}\varphi}

is a two-sided cartesian functor.

Proposition 5.16

(Cocart. functors are closed under Leibniz cotensors)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion. Then, given two-sided cartesian fibrations
\psi:F\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
,
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow C\times D
, and a cocartesian functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle k,m\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

the square induced between the Leibniz cotensors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{Y}\times_{E^{Y}}E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\times B)^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\times B)^{X}\times_{(A\times B)^{Y}}(C\times D)^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\langle k,m\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{X}}

is a cocartesian functor.

Proof.

This works, again, analogously to
[
18
, Proposition 5.3.16]
, using
Proposition

5.14
, and then
Proposition

5.10
.
∎

Corollary 5.17

(Cocartesian functors in a slice are closed under Leibniz cotensors)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion. Then, given two-sided cartesian fibrations
\psi:F\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
,
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
, and a two-sided cartesian functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

the square induced between the Leibniz cotensors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X{\multimap}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y{\multimap}F\times_{Y{\multimap}E}X{\multimap}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}_{A\times B}\kappa}

is a two-sided functor.

In sum, we obtain a synthetic analogue of the cosmological closure properties of two-sided cartesian fibrations, w.r.t. varying as well as a fixed base (cf.
[
68
, Theorem 7.1.4, Proposition 7.1.7, and Definition 7.2.1]
):

Theorem 5.18

((Sliced) cosmological closure properties of two-sided cartesian families)
.

Over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Two-sided cartesian families are closed under composition, dependent products, pullback along arbitrary maps, and cotensoring with maps/shape inclusions. Families corresponding to equivalences or terminal projections are always cocartesian.

Between two-sided cartesian families over Rezk bases, it holds that:
Two-sided cartesian functors are closed under (both horizontal and vertical) composition, dependent products, pullback, sequential limits,
27
27

27

all three objectwise limit notions satisfying the expected universal properties w.r.t. to cocartesian functors
and Leibniz cotensors.

Fibered equivalences and fibered functors into the identity of
\mathbf{1}
are always cocartesian.

Furthermore, all of this is analogously true w.r.t. two-sided cartesian families over the same base and applying sliced versions of the constructions,

6.
Two-sided Yoneda Lemma

In this section we establish a two-sided Yoneda Lemma in fibrational form. It constitutes a type-theoretic version of
[
68
, Theorem 7.3.2]
, after the previous versions for the discrete case
[
66
, Theorems 9.1 and Theorem 9.5]
and the one-sided cocartesian case
[
18
, Theorems 7.2.2 and 7.2.3]
. As explained in the aforementioned sources, in the type-theoretic context, it can be understood as hom-type induction principle for two-sided cartesian type families, analogous to path induction for identity types, cf. e.g.
[
91
, Section 1.12.1]
,
[
74
, Section 5.1]
,
[
65
, September 15: Identity Types]
.

6.1.
Two-sided cartesian sections

Definition 6.1

(Two-sided cartesian sections)
.

Let
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family with associated cocartesian fibration
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
and cartesian fibration
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, resp.

A section
\sigma:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}P(a,b)
is
two-sided cartesian
if it maps pairs
\langle u,\id_{b}\rangle
to
\xi
-cocartesian sections and
\langle\id_{a},v\rangle
to
\pi
-cartesian sections,
i.e.
: for all arrows
u:\Delta^{1}\to A
,
v:\Delta^{1}\to B
and elements
a:A
,
b:B
the dependent arrow
\sigma(u,\id_{b}):\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}P(u(t),b)
is
\xi
-cocartesian while
\sigma(\id_{a},v):\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}P(a,v(t))
is
\pi
-cartesian.

Note that this yields a proposition, and the (sub-)type of such sections is denoted by

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P(a,b)\rightarrowtail \prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}P(a,b).

Of central importance will be the following map. We fix a two-sided family
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
, and assume
a:A
to be initial and
b:B
to be terminal. We then define

\mathbf{y}:P(a,b)\to\prod_{A\times B}P,\quad\mathbf{y}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda d,x,y.(\emptyset_{x})_{!}((!_{y})^{*}d).

Note that by two-sidedness of
P
we have a path

\mathbf{y}(d)(x,y)=(\emptyset_{x})_{!}((!_{y})^{*}b)=(!_{y})^{*}((\emptyset_{x})_{!}d).

In the following, we first show that
\mathbf{y}
is, in fact, valued in two-sided cartesian sections. We then conclude that it is a quasi-inverse of the evaluation map, constituting a quasi-equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{A\times B}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(a,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Finally, the Yoneda Lemmas will follow as instances from this.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family. Assume
a:A
is initial and
b:B
is terminal. Then for all
d:P(a,b)
, the section
\mathbf{y}\,d:\prod_{A\times B} P
is two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

This is an extension of
[
18
, Proposition 7.1.3]
to the two-sided case. We write
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
and fix an element
d:P(a,b)
. Let
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
denote the associated cocartesian fibration. Again, we will only establish one of the two dual parts of the statement, namely that
\mathbf{y}d(u,\id_{y})
is
\xi
-cocartesian for any
u:\Delta^{1}\to A
and
y:B

From this, we define the map that yields the cartesian transport along the terminal maps in
B
,
i.e.
,

\tau\equiv\lambda x,y.(!_{y})^{*}d:A\times B\to E.

Next, consider the family of cocartesian lifts over the initial maps in
A
, starting at the points given by
\tau
. This is realized by the
2
-cell
\tau:\hom_{A\times B\to E}(\tau\,d,\mathbf{y}\,d)
defined by

\tau(x,y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\xi_{!}(\emptyset_{x},(!_{y})^{*}\,d):(!_{y})^{*}d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

.
The action of the
2
-cell
\chi
on a pair
\langle u,\id_{y}\rangle
for
u:\Delta^{1}\to A
and
y:B
is given by the following dependent square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau d(x,y)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}d(x,y)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{(!_{y})^{*}d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}d(u,\id_{y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}

By right cancelation of cocartesian arrows,
\mathbf{y}d(u,\id_{y})
is cocartesian, too.
∎

We need one more lemma before we are ready to prove the main theorem of this subsection, which in turn will specialize to the desired versions of the Yoneda Lemma. The lemma gives canonical identities in the presence of inital and terminal elements, resp., in the base types.

Lemma 6.3

(Coherence of terminal transport with two-sided cartesian sections)
.

Let
A
and
B
types with an initial element
a:A
and terminal element
b:B
. Furthermore, consider a two-sided family
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
with associated cocartesian fibration
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
and cartesian fibration
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, resp. Given a section
\sigma:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P
, for any
x:A
,
y:B
there are identifications

(!_{y})^{*}\sigma(a,b)=\sigma(a,y)\quad\text{and}\quad(\emptyset_{x})_{!}\sigma(a,b)=\sigma(x,b).

Proof.

We only treat the first named case since the second named one is completely dual.

Consider on the one hand the cartesian lift of the terminal map
!_{y}:y\to b
w.r.t.
\sigma(a,b)
(over the identity
\id_{a}:a=a
),
i.e.

Coherence of terminal transport with two-sided cartesian sections

In analogy with
[

, Proposition 7.1.4]
,

, the map
\mathbf{y}:P(a,b)\to\prod_{A\times B}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P
mediates an equivalence between these two types:

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
A,B
be Rezk types with an initial element
a:A
and a terminal element
b:B
. For a two-sided family
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
, evaluation at
\langle a,b\rangle
given by is an equivalence

\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}:\Big(\prod_{A\times B}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}P(a,b)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma(a,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{y})^{*}\sigma(a,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}d(\sigma(a,b))(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(\emptyset_{x},\id_{y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{!}(\emptyset_{x},(!_{y})^{*}\sigma(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Figure 13.
Two-sided cartesian sections

Proof.

We show that for the map
\mathbf{y}
as defined above we have identifications
\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}\circ\mathbf{y}=\id_{P(a,b)}
and
\mathbf{y}\circ\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}=\id_{\prod_{A\times B}P}
. The first case is easy: the initial map into the initial element
a
is just the identity, whose cocartesian lift is an identity as well, and the same holds analogously for the terminal element
b
,
i.e.
,
\mathbf{y}(d)(\sigma)(a,b)=(\emptyset_{a})_{!}(!_{b})^{*}(d)=(\id_{a})_{!}(\id_{b})^{*}(d)=d
.
For the other round-trip, we have to give an identification

(\mathbf{y}\circ\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle})(\sigma)(x,y)=\sigma(x,y)
.
Fix elements
x:A
and
y:B
.
Note that by
Lemma

6.3
there is a path

q:\sigma(a,y)=_{P(a,y)}(!_{y})^{*}(\sigma(a,b))
.
Since
\sigma
is two-sided cartesian, we obtain the dependent square in
Figure

13
. As the filler
g
is cocartesian by right cancelation, and vertical at the same time it is an isomorphism, hence an identity
\sigma(x,y)=\mathbf{y}d(\sigma(a,b))(x,y)
.
∎

6.2.
Dependent and absolute two-sided Yoneda Lemma

Following
[
66
, Theorem 9.5]
and
[
18
, Theorem 7.2.2]
, we obtain the
dependent Yoneda Lemma for two-sided families
by
Proposition

6.4
, and again this will in turn imply the absolute version. Simultaneously, this functions as a type-theoretic version of
[
68
, Theorem 7.3.2]
.

Theorem 6.5

(Dependent Yoneda Lemma for two-sided families)
.

Let
Q:a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. For any
a:A
and
b:
, the evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}_{\id_{\langle a,b\rangle}}:\Big(\prod_{a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}Q\Big)\rightarrow Q(\id_{a},\id_{b})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Recall that by
[
66
, Lemma 8.9]
, the identity map
\id_{a}
is an initial object of the comma type
a\mathbin{\downarrow}A
, while analogously the identity map
\id_{b}
is a terminal object of the cocomma type
B\mathbin{\downarrow}b
. Thus, the claim follows as an instance of
Proposition

6.4
.
∎

We also obtain an absolute analogue in the fashion of
[
66
, Theorem 9.1]
and
[
18
, Theorem 7.2.3]
.

Theorem 6.6

(Absolute Yoneda Lemma for two-sided families,
[
68
, Theorem 7.3.2]
)
.

Let
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. For any
a:A
and
b:B
, the evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}_{\langle\id_{a},\id_{b}\rangle}:\Big(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\\
v:B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\end{subarray}}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P(\partial_{1}\,u,\partial_{0}\,v)\Big)\rightarrow P(a,b)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

The claim follows by setting
Q\equiv\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle^{*}P:a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\to\mathcal{U}
in
Theorem

6.5
. In particular,
Q
is a two-sided fibration again by pullback stability. ∎

7.
Discrete two-sided families

7.1.
Definition and characterization

Definition 7.1

(Two-sided discrete families,
[
66
, Definition 8.28]
)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-variable family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. Then
P
is a
two-sided discrete family
if for all
a:A
,
b:B
the family
P_{b}:A\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant and
P^{a}:B\to\mathcal{U}
is contravariant.

Proposition 7.2

(Two-sided discrete families as discrete objects, cf.
[
68
, Prop. 7.2.4]
)
.

Given
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
over Rezk types, the following are equivalent:

(1)

The family
P
is two-sided discrete.

(2)

The family
P
is both cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right, and every bifiber
P(a,b)
is discrete, for any
a:A
,
b:B
.

Proof.

\ref{it:2s-disc-ii}\implies\ref{it:2s-disc-i}
:

By
Proposition

4.3
,
Item

3
, there is a fibered adjunction which pulls back as follows by
[
18
, Proposition B.2.3]
:
28
28

28

In contrast to the current version of
[
18
, Proposition B.2.3]
one only needs the fibrations involved to be isoinner, and not cocartesian, which in any case becomes clear from the given proof.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi_{b}\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}\times b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

This means exactly that
P^{a}
is covariant. The analogous reasoning establishes the claim for
P_{b}
being covariant since
P
is cartesian on the right, for any
b:B
.
Now, since any
P(a,b)
is discrete, and the fibers of
P_{b}
are given by
P(a,b)
for any
a:A
, we obtain that
P^{a}:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family with discrete fibers, which is equivalent to
P^{a}
being covariant by
[
18
, Corollary 6.1.4]
.

\ref{it:2s-disc-i}\implies\ref{it:2s-disc-ii}
:

The fibrations
P_{a}
and
P^{b}
, resp., being contra- and covariant, resp., imply that all bifibers
P(a,b)
are discrete, for all
a:A
,
b:B
.

Furthermore, for any
b:B
, the family
P^{b}:A\to\mathcal{U}
being cocartesian means that
P^{B}:A\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian, and in addition all
P^{B}
-cocartesian lifts are
P_{A}
-vertical,
i.e.
, lie over an identity in
B
.
29
29

29

This could have also been used as a more direct argument to prove “
\ref{it:2s-disc-ii}\implies\ref{it:2s-disc-i}
” as well.

∎

Corollary 7.3

(Co-/cart. arrows and two-sided cart. functors, cf.
[
68
, Lemma 7.4.3]
)
.

In a two-sided discrete family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
an arrow is
P_{B}
-cocartesian if and only if it is
P^{A}
-vertical. Similarly, an arrow is
P^{A}
-cartesian if and only if it is
P_{B}
-vertical.
In particular, any two-sided discrete cartesian family is two-sided cartesian, and any fibered functor between two-sided discrete families is two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the inspection in the proof of
Proposition

7.2
, together with
[
18
, Proposition 6.1.5]
.

This also establishes that any two-sided discrete cartesian family is, in fact, two-sided cartesian: the commutation condition
Proposition

4.7
is readily verified, because for dependent arrows being vertical (in the respective appropriate sense) is already sufficient for being co-/cartesian, resp.

By naturality, this implies that any fibered functor between two-sided discrete families is two-sided cartesian.
∎

8.
Conclusion

Working in the type theory introduced by Riehl–Shulman
[
66
]
we have developed and investigated a synthetic notion of two-sided cartesian family of
(\infty,1)
-categories. This extends previous work
[
18
]
about the one-sided case. The present work also constitutes a translation of the results from Riehl–Verity’s
\infty
-cosmos theory
[
68
, Chapter 7]
into type theory.

Since we have been able to obtain a lot of the central results, namely characterization theorems, the Yoneda Lemma and several closure properties, we conclude that simplicial homotopy type theory allows to do a fair amount of technically challenging fibered
(\infty,1)
-category theory in a synthetic and more native way. However, in the future it would be desirable, given an appropriate internal construction of the universe types
\mathrm{Cat}
or
\mathrm{Space}
, to capture the theory in a more categorical spirit. E.g., we cannot describe in our type theory a sliced cocartesian fibration
\varphi:\pi\to_{B}\xi
literally as a fibration internal to a Rezk type
\mathrm{Cat}/B
, since the latter has not been defined in this theory in the first place. Thus, our presentation necessarily becomes quite explicit at times somewhat blurring the bigger categorical picture.

Appendix A
Fibered equivalences

We state some expected and useful closure properties of fibered equivalences.

Lemma A.1

(Right properness)
.

Pullbacks of weak equivalences are weak equivalences again,
i.e.
, given a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times_{A}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

then, as indicated if the right vertical map is a weak equivalence, then so is the left hand one.

Proof.

Denote by
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
the straightening of
k:B\to A
, so that
B\simeq\sum_{a:A}P\,a
, and
D\simeq\sum_{c:C}P(j\,c)
. The map
k:B\to A
(identified with its projection equivalence) is a weak equivalence if and only if
\prod_{a:A}\isContr(P\,a)
. This implies
\prod_{c:C}P(j\,c)
which is equivalent to
k^{\prime}=j^{*}k
being a weak equivalence, as desired.
∎

Proposition A.2

(Homotopy invariance of homotopy pullbacks)
.

Given a map between cospans of types

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq~r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq~p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq~q}

where the vertical arrows are weak euivalences the induced map

B\times_{A}C\to B^{\prime}\times_{A^{\prime}}C^{\prime}

is an equivalence as well.

Proof.

By right properness and
2
-out-of-
3
the mediating map
C\to P\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}C^{\prime}\times_{A^{\prime}}A
is an equivalence as can be seen from the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This gives the following cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times_{A}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}\times_{A^{\prime}}B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

and by the Pullback Lemma we know that the back face is a pullback, too. Then again, by right properness

B\times_{A}C\to B^{\prime}\times_{A^{\prime}}C^{\prime}

is an equivalence.
∎

Proposition A.3
.

Given a fibered equivalence as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

and a map
k:A\to B
the fibered equivalence
\varphi
pulls back as shown below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proof.

By projection equivalence, we can consider families
R:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
P:F\equiv\widetilde{R}\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:E\equiv\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
, with
F\equiv\widetilde{Q}
. The fibered equivalence
\varphi
is given by a family of equivalences

\varphi:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
x:R\,b\end{subarray}}\big(\sum_{e:P\,b\,x}Q\,b\,x\,e\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\,b\,x.

The induced family

\varphi^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda a,x.\varphi(k\,a,x):\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
x:R\,k(a)\end{subarray}}\big(\sum_{e:P\,k(a)\,x}Q\,k(a)\,x\,e\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\,k(a)\,x

also constitutes a fibered equivalence. Commutation of all the diagrams is clear since, after projection equivalence, all the vertical maps are given by projections.
∎

Lemma A.4

(Closedness of fibered equivalences under dependent products)
.

Let
I
be a type. Suppose given a family
B:I\to\mathcal{U}
and indexed families
P,Q:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
together with a fiberwise equivalence
\varphi:\prod_{i:I}\prod_{b:B}P_{i}\,b\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{i}\,b
. Then the map

\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}:\big(\prod_{i:I}\widetilde{P_{i}}\big)\longrightarrow_{\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}\big(\prod_{i:I}\widetilde{Q_{i}}\big)

induced by taking the dependent product over
I
is a fiberwise equivalence, too.

Proof.

For
i:I
, we fibrantly replace the given fiberwise equivalence
\varphi_{i}
by projections, giving rise to (strictly) commutative diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{b:B}\sum_{x:Q_{i}(b)}P_{i}(b,x)\simeq E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}\simeq\sum_{b:B}P_{i}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Now,
\varphi
being a fiberwise equivalence is equivalent to

\displaystyle\prod_{i:I}\isEquiv(\varphi_{i})

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{i:I}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B_{i}\\
x:Q_{i}(b)\end{subarray}}\isContr(P_{i}(b,x))

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{\beta:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}\prod_{\sigma:\prod_{i:I}\beta^{*}P_{i}}\prod_{i:I}\isContr\left(P_{i}(\beta(i),\sigma(i))\right).

By (weak) function extensionality,
30
30

30

cf.
[
74
, Theorem 13.1.4(ii)]
, or the discussion at the beginning of
[
66
, Section 4.4.]
this implies

\displaystyle\prod_{\beta:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}\prod_{\sigma:\prod_{i:I}\beta^{*}Q_{i}}\isContr\left(\prod_{i:I}\langle\beta,\sigma\rangle^{*}P_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\isEquiv\left(\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}\right)

wich yields the desired statement. Note, that the latter equivalence follows by projection equivalence of the diagram obtained by applying
\prod_{i:I}(-)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\\
\sigma:\prod_{i:I}\beta^{*}Q_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i:I}\langle\beta,\sigma\rangle^{*}P_{i}\simeq\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\simeq}\sum_{\beta:\prod_{j:I}B_{i}}\prod_{i:I}\beta^{*}Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\xi_{i}}

∎

Lemma A.5

(Closedness of fibered equivalences under sliced products)
.

Given indexed families
P,Q:I\to B\to\mathcal{U}
and a family of fibered equivalences
\prod_{i:I}\prod_{b:B}P_{i}\,b\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{i}\,b
. Then the induced fibered functor

\times_{i:I}^{B}\varphi_{i}:\prod_{i:I}\prod_{b:B}\times_{i:I}^{B}P_{i}\,b\longrightarrow\times_{i:I}^{B}Q_{i}\,b

between the sliced products over
B
is also a fibered equivalence.

Proof.

As usual, denote for
i:
by
\pi_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(P_{i}):E_{i}\to B
and
\xi_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(Q_{i}):F_{i}\to B
the unstraightenings of the given fibered families, giving rise to a (strict) diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}

Since weak equivalences are closed under taking dependent products, the induced fibered map
\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}:\prod_{i:I}E_{i}\to_{I\to B}\prod_{i:I}F_{i}
is also a weak equivalence, and by right properness
Lemma

A.1
the desired mediating map is as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times^{B}_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times^{B}_{i:I}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{B}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

∎

Proposition A.6
.

For an indexing type
I
and a base Rezk type
B
, families of fibered equivalences between Rezk types over
B
are closed under taking sliced products,
i.e.
: Given a family of isoinner fibrations over
B
together with a fibered equivalence as below left, the induced maps on the right make up a fibered equivalence as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}G_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{B}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{B}\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

This is a consequence of
Lemmas

A.4
and
A.5
, considering the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times^{B}_{i:I}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times^{B}_{i:I}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times^{B}_{i:I}G_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}G_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

∎

Appendix B
Fibered (LARI) adjunctions

Building on previous work
[
66
, Section 11]
and
[
18
, Appendix B]
we provide a characterization of fibered LARI adjunctions along similar lines.

Theorem B.1

(Characterizations of fibered adjunctions, cf.
[
66
, Theorem 11.23]
,
[
18
, Theorem B.1.4]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type. For
P,Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
isoinner families we write
\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(P):E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}\twoheadrightarrow B
and
\xi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(Q):F\equiv\widetilde{Q}\twoheadrightarrow B
. Given a fibered functor
\varphi:E\to_{B}F
such that (strictly)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

the following are equivalent propositions:

(1)

The type of
fibered left adjoints
of
\varphi
,
i.e.
, fibered functors
\psi
which are ordinary (transposing) left adjoints of
\varphi
whose unit, moreover, is vertical.

(2)

The type of fibered functors
\psi:F\to_{B}E
together with a vertical
2
-cell

\eta:\id_{F}\Rightarrow_{B}\varphi\psi

s.t.

\Phi_{\eta}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,d,e,k.\varphi_{u}\,k\circ\eta_{d}:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b:B\\
u:a\to b\end{subarray}}\prod_{d:Q\,a,~e:P\,b}\big(\psi_{a}\,d\to^{P}_{u}e\big)\to\big(d\to^{Q}_{u}\varphi_{b}\,e)

is a fiberwise equivalence.

(3)

The type of
sliced
(or
fiberwise
)
left adjoints (over
B
)
to
\varphi
,
i.e.
, fibered functors
\psi:F\to_{B}E
together with a fibered equivalence

\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E\simeq_{F\times_{B}E}F\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\varphi.

(4)

The type of
bi-diagrammatic fibered
(or
fiberwise
)
left adjoints
,
i.e.
, fibered functors
\psi
together with:

•

a vertical natural transformation
\eta:\id_{F}\Rightarrow_{B}\varphi\psi

•

two
vertical
natural transformations
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}:\psi\varphi\Rightarrow_{B}\id_{E}

•

homotopies
31
31

31

by Segal-ness, the witnesses for the triangle identities are actually unique up to contractibility

\alpha:\varphi\varepsilon\circ\eta\varphi=_{E\to F}\id_{\varphi},~\beta:\varepsilon^{\prime}\psi\circ\psi\eta=_{F\to E}\id_{\psi}

(5)

The type of fibered functors
\psi
together with:

•

a vertical natural transformation
\eta:\id_{F}\Rightarrow_{B}\varphi\psi

•

two natural transformations
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}:\psi\varphi\Rightarrow\id_{E}

•

homotopies
\alpha:\varphi\varepsilon\circ\eta\varphi=_{E\to F}\id_{\varphi},~\beta:\varepsilon^{\prime}\psi\circ\psi\eta=_{F\to E}\id_{\psi}

Proof.

At first, we prove that, given a
fixed
and
fibered
functor
\psi:F\to_{B}E
the respective witnessing data are propositions.
32
32

32

This justifies the ensuing list of
logical
equivalences.

\ref{it:fib-ladj-vert-i}\iff\ref{it:fib-ladj-bi-diag-ii}
:

This follows from the equivalence between transposing left adjoint and bi-diagrammatic left adjoint data, cf.
[
66
, Theorem 11.23]
.

\ref{it:fib-ladj-bi-diag-i}\implies\ref{it:fib-ladj-bi-diag-ii}
:

This is clear since the latter is a weakening of the former.

\ref{it:fib-ladj-bi-diag-ii}\implies\ref{it:fib-ladj-bi-diag-i}
:

Denoting the base component of
\varepsilon
by
v:\Delta^{1}\to(B\to B)\simeq B\to(\Delta^{1}\to B)
, projecting down from
\alpha
via
\xi
we obtain the identity
\xi\alpha:v\circ\id_{B}=\id_{B}
. Thus
\varepsilon
is vertical, and similarly one argues for
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

\ref{it:fib-ladj-sliced}\iff\ref{it:fib-ladj-bi-diag-i}
:

Given the fibered functor
\psi
, both lists of data witness that for every
b:B
the components
\psi_{b}\dashv\varphi_{b}:P\,b\to Q\,b
define an adjunction between the fibers, again by
[
66
, Theorem 11.23]
.

\ref{it:fib-ladj-vert-ii}\implies\ref{it:fib-ladj-sliced}
:

The latter is an instance of the former.

\ref{it:fib-ladj-bi-diag-i}\implies\ref{it:fib-ladj-vert-ii}
:

Using naturality and the triangle identities, we show that the fiberwise conditions (vertical case) can be lifted to the case of arbitrary arrows in the base.
33
33

33

We thank Ulrik Buchholtz for pointing out the subsequent argument.

Consider the transposing maps:

\displaystyle\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda k.\varphi_{u}\,k\circ\eta_{d}

\displaystyle:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b:B\\
u:a\to b\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}d:Q\,a\\
e:P\,b\end{subarray}}\big(\psi_{a}\,d\to^{P}_{u}e\big)\to\big(d\to^{Q}_{u}\varphi_{b}\,e\big)

\displaystyle\Psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda m.\varepsilon_{e}\circ\psi_{u}\,m

\displaystyle:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b:B\\
u:a\to b\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}d:Q\,a\\
e:P\,b\end{subarray}}\big(d\to^{Q}_{u}\varphi_{b}\,e)\to\big(\psi_{a}\,d\to^{P}_{u}e\big)

The first roundtrip yields:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(k:\psi_{a}\,d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\varphi_{u}\,k\circ\eta_{d}:d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{b}\,e\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\varepsilon_{e}\circ\psi_{a}(\varphi_{u}\,k\circ\eta_{d}):\psi_{a}\,d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

The result yields back
k
using a triangle identity in the triangle on the left, and naturality of
\varepsilon
in the square on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{a}\,d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\psi\varphi\psi)_{a}\,d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\psi\varphi)_{b}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{a}\,d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{a}\eta_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\psi_{a}\,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{a}\varepsilon_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi\varphi)_{u}\,k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{e}}

In addition, we have also used naturality of
\varepsilon
for
\psi_{a}\varepsilon_{d}\equiv\varepsilon_{\psi_{a}\,d}
. An analogous argument proves the other roundtrip.

We have proven, that relative to a fixed fibered functor
\psi:F\to_{B}E
the different kinds of witnesses that this is a fibered left adjoint to
\varphi
are equivalent propositions, giving rise to a predicate
{\mathrm{isFibLAdj}}_{\varphi}:(F\to_{B}E)\to{\mathrm{Prop}}
. What about the
\Sigma
-type
{\mathrm{FibLAdj}}_{\varphi}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{\psi:F\to_{B}E}{\mathrm{isFibLAdj}}_{\varphi}(\psi)
as a whole? E.g. using the data from
item

3
(after conversion via
[
66
, Theorem 11.23]
), said type is equivalent to

\displaystyle{\mathrm{FibLAdj}}_{\varphi}(\psi)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\psi:\prod_{b:B}P\,b\to Q\,b}\sum_{\eta:\prod_{b:B}\prod_{d:Q\,b}\hom_{Q\,b}(d,(\varphi\,\psi)_{b}\,d)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
d:Q\,b\\
e:P\,b\end{subarray}}\isEquiv\big(\lambda k.\varphi_{b}(k)\circ\eta_{d}\big)

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
d:Q\,b\end{subarray}}\sum_{\psi_{b}:P\,b}\sum_{\eta_{d}:d\to_{Q\,b}\varphi_{b}(\psi_{b}d)}\prod_{e:P\,b}\isEquiv(\lambda k.\varphi_{b}(k)\circ\eta_{d}).

Finally, one shows that this is indeed a proposition, completely analogously to the argument given in the proof of
[
66
, Theorem 11.23]
for the non-dependent case.
∎

Definition B.2

(Fibered (LARI) adjunction)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
,
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow B
be isoinner fibrations, with
P\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{B}(\pi)
and
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{B}(\xi)
. Given a fibered functor
\varphi:E\to_{B}F
, the data of a
fibered left adjoint right inverse (LARI) adjunction
is given by

•

a fibered functor
\psi:F\to_{B}E
,

•

and an equivalence
\Phi:\psi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E\simeq_{F\times_{B}E}F\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\varphi
s.t. the fibered unit

\eta_{\Phi}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b,d.\Phi_{b,d,\psi_{b}\,d}(\id_{\psi_{b}\,d}):\prod_{b:B}\prod_{d:Q\,b}d\to_{Q\,b}(\varphi\psi)_{b}(d)

is a componentwise homotopy.

Together, this defines the data of a
fibered LARI adjunction
. Diagrammatically, we represent this by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

In fact, as established in the previous works of
[
66
, Section 11]
the unit of a coherent adjunction is determined uniquely up to homotopy. Hence, using the characterizations of a (coherent) LARI adjunction, the type of fibered LARI adjunctions in the above sense is equivalent to the type of LARI adjunctions which are also fibered adjunctions. This implies the validity of the familiar closure properties for this restricted class as well.

Appendix C
Sliced commas and products

We record here explicitly some closure properties involving sliced commas and products that are often used, especially in the treatise of two-sided fibrations and related notions.

Proposition C.1

(Dependent products of sliced commas)
.

For a type
I
and
i:I
, given fibered cospans

\psi_{i}:F_{i}\to_{B_{i}}G_{i}\leftarrow_{B_{i}}E_{i}:\varphi_{i}

of Rezk types, taking the dependent product fiberwisely commutes with forming sliced comma types:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{i}\downarrow_{B_{i}}\varphi_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}(\psi_{i}\downarrow_{B_{i}}\varphi_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\prod_{i:I}\psi_{i}\right)\downarrow_{\left(\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\right)}\left(\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proof.

We denote by
P_{i},Q_{i},R_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
the straightenings of the given maps
E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}
,
F_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}
, and
G_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}
, resp.
Using projection equivalence, the sliced commas are computed as

(17)

\displaystyle\psi_{i}\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B_{i}}\varphi_{i}\simeq\sum_{b:B_{i}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e:P_{i}(b)\\
d:Q_{i}(b)\end{subarray}}\big(\psi_{i}\big)_{b}(d)\longrightarrow_{R_{i}(b)}\big(\varphi_{i}\big)_{b}(e).

From this and the type-theoretic axiom of choice, we obtain as projection equivalence for

\prod_{i:I}(\psi_{i}\downarrow_{B_{i}}\varphi_{i})\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{i:I}B_{i}

the type

\displaystyle\prod_{i:I}(\psi_{i}\downarrow_{B_{i}}\varphi_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(\ref{eq:fam-sliced-comma})}}}{{\simeq}}\prod_{i:I}\sum_{b:B_{i}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e:P_{i}(b)\\
d:Q_{i}(b)\end{subarray}}\big(\psi_{i}\big)_{b}(d)\longrightarrow_{R_{i}(b)}\big(\varphi_{i}\big)_{b}(e)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(AC)}}}{{\simeq}}\sum_{\beta:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}\prod_{i:I}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e:P_{i}(\beta(i))\\
d:Q_{i}(\beta(i))\end{subarray}}\big(\psi_{i}\big)_{\beta(i)}(d)\longrightarrow_{R_{i}(\beta(i))}\big(\varphi_{i}\big)_{\beta(i)}(e)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(\ref{eq:fam-sliced-comma})}}}{{\simeq}}\sum_{\beta:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma:\prod_{i:I}P_{i}(\beta(i))\\
\tau:\prod_{i:I}Q_{i}(\beta(i))\end{subarray}}\big(\prod_{i:I}\psi(\tau)\big)\to_{\big(\prod_{i:I}R_{i}(\beta(i))\big)}\big(\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}(\sigma)\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(AC)}}}{{\simeq}}\left(\prod_{i:I}\psi_{i}\right)\downarrow_{\left(\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\right)}\left(\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}\right)

This yields the desired fibered equivalence.
∎

Corollary C.2

(Products of commas in a slice)
.

Fix a base Rezk type
B
be and an indexing type
I
. Given for
i:I
an isoinner fibration
\pi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B
consider a cospan of isoinner fibrations
\psi_{i}:F_{i}\to E_{i}\leftarrow G_{i}:\varphi
. Then we have a fibered equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}\big(\psi_{i}\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\varphi_{i}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\times_{i:I}^{B}\varphi_{i}\right)\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\left(\times_{i:I}^{B}\psi_{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\times_{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\right)\times_{I\to B}\left(\prod_{i:I}E_{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proposition C.3

(Fibered (LARI) adjunctions are preserved by sliced products)
.

For an indexing type
I
and a base Rezk type
B
, families of fibered (LARI) adjunctions between Rezk types over
B
are closed under taking sliced products,
i.e.
: Given a family of isoinner fibrations over
B
together with a fibered (LARI) adjunction as below left, the
induced maps on the right make up a fibered (LARI) adjunction as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{B}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{B}\psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

Given a family of fibered adjunctions as indicated amounts to a family of fibered equivalences, themselves fibered over
B
, for
i:I
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{i}\downarrow_{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{i}\downarrow_{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}\times_{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Taking the dependent product over
i:I
produces a fibered equivalence, itself fibered over
B^{I}
. Pullback along
\mathrm{cst}:B\to B^{I}
yields the sliced products and again preserves the fibered equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\times_{i:I}^{B}\psi_{i}\downarrow_{B}E_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}\psi_{i}\downarrow_{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\times_{i:I}^{B}F_{i}\downarrow_{B}\varphi_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\downarrow_{B}\varphi_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\times_{i:I}^{B}F_{i}\times_{B}E_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}F_{i}\times_{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Since sliced products canonically commute with both sliced commas and fiber products, this gives a fibered equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\times_{i:I}^{B}\psi_{i}\big)\downarrow_{I\to B}\big(\times_{i:I}^{B}E_{i}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\times_{i:I}^{B}F_{i}\big)\downarrow_{I\to B}\big(\times_{i:I}^{B}\varphi_{i}\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\times_{i:I}^{B}F_{i}\big)\times_{I\to B}\big(\times_{i:I}^{B}E_{i}\big)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

which exactly yields the desired fibered adjunction of the sliced products.
∎
